Local limit of the focusing discrete NLS
Kesav Krishnan, Gourab Ray

Abstract
We examine the behavior of a function sampled from the invariant measure associated to the focusing discrete Non Linear Schrödinger equation, defined on a discrete torus of dimension d ⩾ 3 d\geqslant 3 , and nonlinearity parameter p > 4 p>4 , in the infinite volume limit. The Gibbs measure has two parameters, the inverse temperature and the strength of the non linearity, and the scaling is such that the non linear and linear parts of the Hamiltonian contribute on the same scale. It was shown by Dey, Kirkpatrick and the first author that this measure undergoes a phase transition, concerning concentration of mass of a typical function at the level of partition functions. We prove that the three regions of the phase diagram yield three distinct local limits, a massive Gaussian free field, a massless Gaussian free field plus a random constant, and finally a (possibly trivial) mixture of massive Gaussian free fields, where some mass is “lost” to the region of concentration. Our proof relies on the analysis of the spherical model of a ferromagnet. The measure under consideration is an exponential tilt of the spherical model, and the additional tilt can be seen to induce an additional phase transition. Although our motivations come from the NLS equation and soliton resolution conjecture, our proofs are completely probabilistic, and can be read without any knowledge of PDE theory.



1
Introduction and main results

The nonlinear Schrödinger equation (or
NLS
in short) is a prototypical example of a nonlinear dispersive PDE and has attracted significant attention over the years (see
[
24
]
and references therein for a broad overview on the study of nonlinear dispersive equations). Constructing invariant Gibbs measures for this PDE and studying the typical functions sampled from these measures is an important problem in this field with several influential works that address this
[
17
,
4
,
5
,
27
,
21
,
26
,
6
]
.
Our main concern in this article is to study the behavior of the invariant Gibbs measure associated with this PDE in the discrete setting of the
d
-dimensional torus.

Specifically, we work on the torus
\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}:=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{d}
, with
N:=n^{d}.

Fix
p>4
and
\nu,\theta>0
.
Let us introduce the notation

(1.1)

\nu_{N}:=\frac{2}{p}\left(\frac{\nu}{N}\right)^{{}^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}.

Our main protagonist is the random variable
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}:\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
distributed according to the following law. For any bounded continuous function
\mathfrak{f}
define

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N})):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\exp\left[\theta\left(\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\mathbf{d}\psi.

where
\|\psi\|_{p}^{p}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}}|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})|^{p}
and
\|\nabla\psi\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})-\psi(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}
, where
\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{y}
denotes the vertices being adjacent.
We call the random vector
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
as defined in
Equation

1.2
a
focusing NLS field
with parameters
\nu,\theta
and the measure induced by it the
focusing NLS invariant measure
, or simply the
focusing NLS measure
, denoted by
\mu_{N,\nu,\theta}
. The reasoning behind this nomenclature is that this measure is an invariant measure for the discrete focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation, we shall explain this connection later in
Section

1.2
.
For now, readers familiar with the Gaussian free field (GFF) (see
Section

2
for an overview) will recognize (
1.2
) as an exponential tilt the complex GFF at temperature
\theta
by the term
\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\|\psi\|_{p}^{p})
. Furthermore, since
\nu_{N}
is positive, the restriction of the
\ell^{2}
-norm is introduced to ensure the integral is finite (so we have a probability measure).

The reasoning for the form of the tilt coefficient
\nu_{N}
is to match the order of the Gaussian tail with the tilt, the details will be clearer later.
Observe for now that we could have introduced an extra parameter
\gamma
in the indicator above and restricted to
\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\gamma N
instead of
\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N
. However, calling this random field
\Psi^{\nu,\theta,\gamma}
, and using the invariance

\Psi^{k\nu,k\theta,k^{-1}\gamma}_{N}=\Psi^{\nu,\theta,\gamma}_{N}\text{ in law,}

it is sufficient to concentrate on
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
as defined in
Equation

1.2
. Recall that the local weak limit of a random field
X_{N}:\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
is
X:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
if for every finite set
A
,
(X_{N})_{x\in A}
converges to
(X)_{x\in A}
.

Fix
d\geqslant 3
and define

(1.3)

C_{d}={\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(0,0)<\infty,

where
{\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
denotes the Green’s function in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Our main result, censoring some of the details, is summarized as follows. We postpone the formal definition of the massive Gaussian free field to
2.1
, for now it suffices to recall that it is a Gaussian field on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
whose correlations are given by the massive Green’s function, which decay exponentially in the distance if the mass is strictly positive. If the mass is zero, this decay is polynomial.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
p>4
and
d\geqslant 3
.
For every
\theta>0
there exists a
\nu_{c}=\nu_{c}(\theta)\in[0,\infty)
such that the following holds. All the GFFs mentioned below are complex GFFs with variance
\theta^{-1}
.

a.

If
\theta<C_{d}
,
\nu<\nu_{c}
, then the local weak limit of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
is
\theta^{-1/2}
times a massive GFF with mass
m(\theta)>0
given by (
3.3
).

b.

If
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
and
\nu<\nu_{c}
, then the local weak limit if
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
is
\theta^{-1/2}\cdot
GFF(with no mass)
+U\boldsymbol{1}
where
U\in\mathbb{C}
is uniform over a disc of radius
\sqrt{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}
centered around
0
and and
\boldsymbol{1}
is the function which is identically 1 on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In particular, if
\theta=C_{d}
then
U=0
almost surely.

c.

If
\nu>\nu_{c}
, then subsequential weak limit exists and each such limit is
\theta^{-1/2}
times a massive GFF with a potentially random mass
M
. Furthermore,
M>0
almost surely, and if
\theta<C_{d}
then
M<m(\theta)
almost surely, where
m(\theta)
is as in item a.

Figure 1:
The phase diagram for NLS field. The purple region corresponds the massive GFF limit for supercritical
\nu
(
1.1
, part c.), the red region corresponds to the massive GFF limit for subcritical
\nu
(
1.1
, part a.), and the green region corresponds to the (non-massive) GFF with the random global shift limit (
1.1
, part b.) We do not know of the behaviour of the field at the critical curve (drawn in solid brown), see
Section

7
for further discussions. Something curious happens if we fix
\nu
to correspond to the horizontal dashed red line. As we vary
\theta
, the correlations are decaying exponentially at first (red part), then polynomially (green part), and then exponentially again (purple part).

A more precise version of item c. above can be found in
5.4
.
We believe that the random variable
M
above is actually deterministic and is a subject of future work; more details follow later.
To explain
1.1
in greater detail, we introduce an important supporting character in this article, the
spherical model
. We define the
spherical law
to be the law of the random field
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}:\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
defined as follows. For every bounded continuous function
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
we have

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta})):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\exp\left(-\theta\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\mathbf{d}\psi.

Clearly, the NLS model is a tilt of the spherical model.
More precisely,

(1.5)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}))=\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}\mathbb{E}(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}\|_{p}^{p})\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta})),

and the spherical model, in turn, is a GFF conditioned to have its total
\ell^{2}
-mass restricted. To compare the behavior of the spherical model with that of the NLS, we mention here that the spherical model also undergoes a phase transition.

Theorem 1.2
.

The following holds.

a.

If
\theta<C_{d}
,
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}
converges locally to
\theta^{-1/2}
times a massive GFF with mass
m(\theta)
, which is the same as item a. in
1.1
.

b.

If
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
, then the local weak limit if
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}
is
\theta^{-1/2}
GFF(with no mass)
+U\boldsymbol{1}
where
U
is uniform over a disc of radius
\sqrt{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}
centered at
\boldsymbol{0}
and and
\boldsymbol{1}
is the function which is identically 1 on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In particular, if
\theta=C_{d}
then
U=0
almost surely.

The spherical model defined with the
\ell^{2}
-norm being equal to
N
, as opposed
\ell^{2}
-norm less than or equal to
N
considered in this article has a rich history. It was originally introduced by Berlin and Kac
[
3
]
as a model of ferromagnetism more amenable to analysis by the method of steepest descent. In particular, the limiting free energy was shown to match with a that of a corresponding Gaussian Free Field. A detailed picture of the low temperature regime for the case of complex valued spins was established by Lukkarinen in
[
18
]
, with a specific emphasis on the nature of the “condensate” (The Fourier modes corresponding to the small eigenvalues) as the dispersion relation varies, and the convergence of the “normal fluid” (the Fourier modes corresponding to the orthogonal complement of the condensate) to a critical Gaussian field. During the preparation of our article, Aru and Korzhenkova put forth an article
[
2
]
which characterized the local limit of the spherical model for real valued spins, as well as the spin
O(N)
model with simultaneous limit of large spin dimensionality, the major advancement being the rigorous proof of the local limit in the critical case
\theta=C_{d}
.

Our main focus is the local limit of the NLS field, of which
1.2
is an important ingredient. There is a significant overlap between Theorem
1.2
and the main theorem of
[
2
]
as well as a subcase of the main result of
[
18
]
. In particular, the
\theta>C_{d}
case can be deduced from the methods of
[
2
]
, and our technique here is an alternative. However, there are notable differences which arise from the differences in the constraint. The “
\leqslant
” constraint allows for significantly simplified proofs in the
\theta=C_{d}
case. Additionally, in the
\theta<C_{d}
case, we also require the local limit when boundary conditions are imposed, which is not covered by
[
2
]
or
[
18
]
. Our proof techniques follow somewhat diverging paths, proceeding via only analyzing the Fourier modes, more details on this in
Section

6
. We remark however that we do use the sharpened Green’s function estimates verified in
[
2
]
. However, we only need a weak version of this estimate for
d\geqslant 4
. Furthermore, we do not need the precise sign of the difference of the finite and infinite volume Green’s functions in
d=3
.

With the aid of
1.2
, the effect of the tilt is apparent. If
\nu>\nu_{c}
, then item c. of
1.1
tells us that some mass from the local limit of the spherical model is ‘lost’. In contrast if
\nu<\nu_{c}
, then no mass is ‘lost’ and the behavior is similar to that of the spherical model, and in fact identical in the thermodynamic limit. In fact, the tilt induces the following “double” phase transition on the spherical model.

Corollary 1.3
.

There exists a
\nu>0
such that
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
undergoes two phase transitions as
\theta
is varied, the first at
\theta=C_{d}
and the second at
\nu_{c}^{-1}(\theta)>C_{d}
, where the local limit is first massive, then non massive, then finally massive again (see
Figure

1
)

In particular, what this means is that the decay of correlations for
\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}
as
\theta
is increased is first exponential, then polynomial, and finally exponential again.

1.1
Heuristics behind the phase transition

Let us now explain the heuristics behind
1.1
, particularly, the reason behind the appearance of mass in the GFF, and its subsequent ‘loss’ for large
\nu
. This program was initiated by the first author along with Dey and Kirkpatrick in
[
13
]
which forms the basis of this work. The key idea is that large values of
\nu
forces the focusing NLS field to concentrate
O(N)
amount of
\ell^{2}
mass in a small region (the exact connection with the NLS equation and soliton formation is explained later in
Section

1.2
). To that end, let us consider the (random) set
U
where absolute value of the NLS field is at least
\sqrt{\varepsilon N}
. If we assume the total
\ell^{2}
-mass concentrated on
U
is
aN
, then the NLS field on
U
should concentrate around the minimal energy field on
U
constrained to have mass
aN
. This minimal energy is captured by the function
I
which we define below in (
1.8
).

Note that because of the
\ell^{2}
-constraint,
U
is not too large, only of order roughly
\varepsilon^{-1}
in size. It is not hard to see (
Lemma

4.1
) that the largest
\ell^{p}
-norm of a function with constraints in both
\ell^{2}
and
\ell^{\infty}
norm is obtained by assigning the largest possible absolute values to as many vertices as possible. Obviously, the largest number of vertices where we can assign absolute value
\sqrt{\varepsilon N}
, if the
\ell^{2}
norm is bounded by
N
, is given by
\varepsilon^{-1}
. Thus the maximum
\ell^{p}
-norm with the given constraints is
\varepsilon^{-1}(\varepsilon N)^{p/2}
. Overall the tilt term is

\exp\left(\frac{2}{p}(\nu\varepsilon)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}N\right)

Thus if
\varepsilon>0
is small enough, the tilt term is insignificant in exponential scale, and the dominant term is the partition function of the spherical model with mass at most
(1-a)N
. This contribution (which we call the contribution of the entropy) is dictated by the log partition function of a GFF whose
\ell^{2}
-mass is constrained. This is captured by the function
W
which we define below. The main result of
[
13
]
is to show that the log partition function of the NLS field concentrates on a value of
a
which minimizes the sum of the contribution from
I
and
W
.

Now let us define the terms
I
and
W
more concretely, the above heuristics should demystify the motivation behind these definitions.
Let us define energy of a function
\psi_{N}:\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi_{N})
as

(1.6)

\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi_{N}):=\left\|\nabla\psi_{N}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\nu_{N}\left\|\psi_{N}\right\|_{p}^{p}.

The reasoning behind calling it ‘energy’ should be clear by recalling the Hamiltonian in the NLS measure (
1.2
).
The minimum energy at (rescaled)
\ell^{2}
mass
a
is denoted

(1.7)

\displaystyle I_{N}(aN):=\inf\left\{\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi_{N}):\psi_{N}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}),\left\|\psi_{N}\right\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}=aN\right\}.

It is known (see
[
13
]
and
[
10
]
) that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}I_{N}(aN)=I(a),

where
I(a)
can be characterized as the minimum energy with
\ell^{2}
mass
a
in infinite volume as follows:

(1.8)

\displaystyle I(a):=\inf\left\{\mathcal{H}(\psi):\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z}^{d}),\left\|\psi\right\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}=a\right\}.

where

\mathcal{H}(\psi)=\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{p}\nu^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}.

Note the (appriximate) scaling relation

``\mathcal{H}(\psi_{N}/\sqrt{N})=\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi_{N})^{\prime\prime}

where the approximation comes from the fact that the periodic boundary conditions are somewhat incompatible near the boundary for the energy computation. We don’t need the exact scaling relation in what follows.

As for the function
W
, let
\Delta
denote the graph Laplacian on the discrete torus, defined as

(1.9)

(\Delta\psi)(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\sim\boldsymbol{x}}\psi(\boldsymbol{y})-\deg(\boldsymbol{x})\psi(\boldsymbol{x}),\qquad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}.

where
\deg(\boldsymbol{x})
is the degree of
\boldsymbol{x}
, and
\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{y}
denotes the
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{y}
being adjacent.
We point out to the astute reader that sometimes the graph Laplacian is defined as the above quantity divided by
\deg(x)
, which might be a source of confusion with normalization in other references.
Now define

(1.10)

W_{N}(b):=\frac{1}{N}\log\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\exp(-\|\nabla\psi_{N}\|^{2}_{2})1_{\|\psi_{N}\|^{2}_{2}\leqslant bN}d\psi_{N}

and

(1.11)

W(b):=\lim_{N\to\infty}W_{N}

The fact that the above limit exists is proved in
[
13
]
. Let

(1.12)

F_{\nu,\theta}(a)=F(a):=W(\theta(1-a))+\frac{\theta}{\nu}I(\nu a);\qquad{a\in[0,1]}.

and define
F_{\min}=\min\{F(a):a\in[0,1]\}
. It is proved in
[
13
]
*Theorem 2.3 that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)=\log(\frac{\pi}{\theta})-F_{\min}.

We will later need a more quantitative version of the above convergence in
Section

4.1
.
It was proved in
[
13
]
*Lemmas 4.7, 5.9 that
W
and
I
are continuous functions in
[0,\infty)
. Define

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu):=\argmin_{0\leqslant a\leqslant 1}\left(F(a)\right)

as the set of minimizers for the variational formula. This set is compact by the continuity of
W
and
I
.

Going back to the heuristics with the set
U
, the splitting of mass on
U
and
U^{c}
is dictated by
Equation

1.13
. It turns out that
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)\ni 0
if
\nu<\nu_{c}
and
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
is bounded away from 0 if
\nu>\nu_{c}
. The former is called the
dispersive phase
and the latter the
solitonic phase
(more on this in
Section

1.2
). Furthermore, if
\nu>\nu_{c}
,
\theta(1-a)<C_{d}
for every
a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
which explains the positive mass of the local limit GFF in
1.1
, item c.

Note however that the analysis in
[
13
]
is mostly at the level of partition functions, and not at the level of the distribution of the NLS field itself. Therefore, several technical hurdles need to be crossed to make the above heuristic precise, which is what we accomplish in this manuscript.

1.2
Motivations: Solitions and NLS equation

Although we do not directly deal with the associated PDE in this article, it is worthwhile explaining the PDE perspective. To draw analogues with the continuum, we need to introduce a parameter
h
which is equal to the
lattice spacing
, or the distance between nearest neighbours of
\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The focusing discrete NLS now with nonlinearity parameter
p
is given by

(1.14)

{\mathsf{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi(\boldsymbol{x},t)=-\frac{1}{h^{2}}\Delta\psi(\boldsymbol{x},t)-|\psi(\boldsymbol{x},t)|^{p-2}\psi(\boldsymbol{x},t),\qquad\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d},\quad t>0

where
\mathsf{i}=\sqrt{-1}
.
Without the nonlinear term
|\psi(\boldsymbol{x},t)|^{p-2}\psi(\boldsymbol{x},t)
, (
1.14
) is known as the free Schrödinger equation which can be solved explicitly, by diagonalizing the Laplacian using the Fourier basis. The free Schrödinger equation is
dispersive
, and one of the consequences is that the
\ell^{q}
norm of the solution locally tends to 0 as
t\to\infty
, for any
q\in(2,\infty]
.

The term
focusing
refers to the negative sign in front of the nonlinear term, which in a very precise sense combats the dispersive effect of the linear evolution. A unique feature of the focusing DNLS is the fact that it admits
soliton
solutions, which arise explicitly as a consequence of the competition between the dispersion and non linearity. These are stable, time periodic, spatially preserved solutions to (
1.14
) Changing this sign leads to the
defocusing
variant of this equation, where the non linearity in a sense aids the dispersion.

The DNLS equation (
1.14
) admits a Hamiltonian of the form

(1.15)

\mathcal{H}(\psi)=\frac{1}{Nh^{2}}\|\nabla\psi\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{p\cdot N}\|\psi\|_{p}^{p},

where
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
are the canonical conjugate variables. By Nöether’s theorem (see
[
24
]
*Section 1.5), both the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
and the mass
\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}
are conserved in time under the DNLS flow. By Liouville’s theorem,
[
24
]
*Exercise 1.33, the Lebesgue measure on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n})
is also invariant with respect to the flow. Consequently, the following Gibbs measure

(1.16)

\mu_{\beta,B}(\psi)\propto\exp(-\beta\mathcal{H}(\psi))1_{\|\psi\|^{2}_{2}\leqslant BN}

is invariant with respect to the DNLS flow (more precisely, if we consider
(\Psi(\boldsymbol{x},0))_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}\sim\mu_{\beta,B}
and let it evolve using (
1.16
), then
(\Psi(\boldsymbol{x},t))_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}\sim\mu_{\beta,B}
as well for any
t>0
.)

The Gibbs measure in (
1.16
) was first analyzed by Chatterjee and Kirpatrick in
[
9
]
for
p=4
and
d\geqslant 3
. Chatterjee extended this result to a parameter regime where the gradient term is non-negligible but only for
p<2+4/d
which yields a non-trivial scaling limit
[
10
]
. It is also worthwhile to note that the result in
[
10
]
is not for a Gibbs measure of the form
eq.

1.14
, but from a ‘uniformly chosen’ random field with given mass and energy properly chosen. The scaling regime considered in this article (and first introduced in
[
13
]
) is distinct from either of these two cases. As observed in
[
9
]
, in the limit
N\to\infty
and
h\to 0
slowly such that the “side length”
N^{\frac{1}{d}}h
remains bounded away from
0
or diverges, the non linear term dominates the dispersion. This can be seen by evaluating the Hamiltonian at a function
\phi
which takes value
\sqrt{BN}
on a single lattice site. Observe that

\frac{2}{pN}\left\|\phi\right\|_{p}^{p}=\frac{2N^{\frac{p-2}{2}}B^{\frac{p}{2}}}{p}\text{ and }\frac{1}{Nh^{2}}\left\|\nabla\phi\right\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{2dB}{h^{2}}.

When
p>4
, the non linearity not only beats the dispersion, but in fact dominates the entropy associated to the uniform measure on the
\ell^{2}
ball, and there is no phase transition. The scaling of
N^{-\frac{p-2}{2}}
introduced in front of the non linearity in this article and
[
13
]
is meant to combat this, such that the linear and non linear parts of the Hamiltonian always contribute on the same scale. The scaling in
[
10
]
is also such that the two parts contribute on the same scale, however the condition that
p<2+\frac{4}{d}
is crucial. When
d\geqslant 3
and
p>4
, we are in a regime of scaling where the continuum limits of discrete solitons diverge in the appropriate norms, which removes the possibility of considering the analogous continuum scaling in
[
10
]
.

We can relate the parameters
\nu,\theta,N
of the NLS field (
1.2
) to the parameters
\beta,B,h,N
that appear in (
1.16
). This is simply given by

(1.17)

\theta=\frac{\beta B}{Nh^{2}}\text{ and }\nu_{N}=h^{2}B^{\frac{p-2}{2}}.

Our result is thus a natural addition to the results in
[
13
,
10
,
9
]
, we work with a parameter setup which yields a non-trivial discrete limit.

The study of the discrete NLS stems in part to understand qualitative aspects of the continuum equation. The continuum focusing NLS displays subtle behavior in terms of well-posedness and depends crucially on
p
and the regularity of the initial data, see Chapter 14 of
[
25
]
and
[
24
]
for a broader overview, and there remain many regimes where the long term behavior of solutions is poorly understood. In particular, there is a drastic difference in the requirements for well posedness when
p<2+\frac{4}{d}
and
p\geqslant 2+\frac{4}{d}
, and the finite time blow up phenomenon for large
p
has an analogue in discrete regime, in the form of a threshold for soliton formation
[
28
]
.

There is a rich collection of literature on the construction of continuum invariant measures for the NLS, as well as their uses in addressing questions such as well-posedness. The typical approach here is to construct exponential tilts of an appropriate Gaussian Free field, and showing that the tilt is integrable. For the defocusing NLS, invariant measures were used by Bourgain to prove global well-posedness for the cubic NLS in
d=1

[
4
]
and
d=2

[
5
]
on tori, and later by Tzvetkov for the NLS defined on the disk in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

[
27
]
. Recently, Bourgain’s work in
d=2
was significantly extended beyond cubic nonlinearity by Deng, Nahmod and Yue
[
11
]
. We also point out a remarkable phenomenon, the use of randomly sampled initial data to construct solutions in regularity regimes that are deterministically ill-posed, identified by Tzvetkov and Burq
[
7
,
8
]
. It is worth noting that the non linearity terms are Wick ordered in
d>1
, since the underlying Gaussian free field is supported on distributions which cannot be evaluated at points.

The focusing case has also been examined, particularly in the regime
p<2+\frac{4}{d}
, where
H^{1}
initial data is adequate to ensure a global solution. This was initiated by Lebowitz, Rose and Speer on the 1-dimensional torus
[
17
]
. This measure was proved to be invariant for the focusing NLS by McKean and Vaninsky
[
19
]
, as well as Bourgain
[
4
]
. The cases of
p=2+\frac{4}{d}
are more subtle, and normalizability (finiteness of the partition function) holds in
d=1
upto and including a critical mass threshold as proved by Oh, Sosoe and Tolomeo
[
20
]
, but not in
d=2

[
6
]
for any mass cut off as proved by Brydges and Slade. The approach of tilting the Gaussian Free Field fails even with Wick ordering in
d\geqslant 3
, as the field is too rough, which is why the approach of discretization has been adopted in this article, as well as in
[
9
]
and
[
13
]
.

Understanding the behavior of a typical function from the continuum focusing NLS measure as the volume tends to infinity is a problem that is quite involved, and has attracted a great deal of interest. Early results on the triviality of the limit when
p=4
and
d=1
are due to Rider
[
21
]
, particularly that there is a strong concentration of the measure about a continuum soliton of appropriate mass, which can be freely translated. This was significantly expanded upon by Tolomeo and Weber in
[
26
]
, again for
p=4
and
d=1
by tuning the strength of the non linearity and examining the corresponding regimes. In particular, there is a critical regime where a phase transition similar to the one considered here and in
[
13
]
emerges, and the subcritical limiting measure is Gaussian. Corresponding to the strongly non linear case for the same parameters, Seong and Sosoe recently verified a central limit theorem for fluctuations of a the typical function around the soliton, namely that they converge to white noise
[
23
]
. Our result here in a precise sense is the higher dimensional analogue of
[
26
]
and
[
23
]
for the regime of critical strength of non linearity and
p>4
. It would be interesting to see whether our results can be extended to study the fluctuations in the vicinity of the discrete soliton like
[
23
]
, but difficulties such as verifying local convexity of the Hamiltonian remain. From a rather different perspective, understanding the infinite volume limit of an invariant measure for the focusing NLS led Chatterjee to a proof of a version of the
soliton resolution conjecture

[
10
]
for general
p<2+4/d
, which roughly states that for generic initial data, after sufficient time has elapsed a solution should be composed of a soliton and a portion that disperses away, and invariant measures are a natural means of characterizing the term “generic”. Although still in finite volume, we also direct attention towards the work of Greco, Oh, Tolomeo and L. Tao
[
16
]
for the case of “weak” non-linearity in
d=2
, where the strength of the non linearity term is sent to zero, which also results in a phase transition.

1.3
Outline of the proof

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is split into two parts, corresponding to subcritical and supercritical
\nu
for a given
\theta
. Common to both is the characterization of the phases of the measure, as put forth in
[
13
]
. Namely, we deterministically define a (possibly empty) set

U(\psi,\varepsilon):=\{\boldsymbol{x}:|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})|>\sqrt{\varepsilon N}\}

for some threshold
\varepsilon
(to be carefully chosen later). Because of the
\ell^{2}
-bound on
\psi
, this set is of size
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
, again deterministically. As mentioned before, the mass of the NLS field on
U
concentrates on a minimizer of the energy functional
F
(
1.12
), and on the rest the
\ell^{\infty}
norm is
o(\sqrt{N})
.
We need
U
to satisfy some more technical properties, which is recapped again in Section
4.1
, but let us ignore those for now. This statement is fleshed out in
Proposition

4.6
, which we call
tempering the NLS field.

One of the main results of
[
13
]
is that
\frac{1}{N}\left\|\psi|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}
jumps discontinuously from
0
to
>0
as
\nu
is changed from subcritical to supercritical, however this was only done through the lens of partition functions, which is not enough for the results in this article. The key to the actual behaviour of
U
is an analysis of the energy functional
F
, which is done in
Section

4.2
. There are certain gaps in our knowledge of the behaviour of
F
, and indeed once those are filled, the techniques of our paper can be pushed through to give finer versions of
1.1
, particularly in the case
\nu\geqslant\nu_{c}
.

In the subcritical case (
\nu<\nu_{c}
), the goal is to show that the local limit of
\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}
is the same as
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}
. Broadly, this is accomplished in two steps:

1.

We modify the proof of convergence of free energy in
[
13
]
to obtain large deviation estimates for
\left\|\Psi|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, namely Proposition
4.6
. These estimates enable us to deduce an effective
\ell^{\infty}
bound for
\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}
, namely that with high probability
\left\|\Psi\right\|_{\infty}
is
o(\sqrt{N})
. The verification for the
\ell^{\infty}
bounds are distinct for
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
and
\theta>C_{d}
, the latter requires an additional application of a discrete version of the GNS inequality.

2.

Verify a gaussian tail estimate for
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}
, and use it to upgrade the previous
\ell^{\infty}
bound to
O(N^{\beta})
, where
0<\beta<1/2
, and is small enough such that any function with this
\ell^{\infty}
norm has
\ell^{p}
which is
o(N^{\frac{p-2}{2}})
. This allows us to disregard the exponential tilt.

All of this is accomplished in
Section

5.1
.

The supercritical case
(\nu>\nu_{c})
is more involved, and essentially involves the removal of the region of concentration, followed by an application of a version of the subcritical argument. The order of events are as follows.

1.

Separate out the set of concentration, yielding an
\ell^{\infty}
bound outside
U
. This also removes a (possibly random) but strictly non zero fraction of the mass. This involves the large deviation estimates for
\left\|\Psi|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}
mentioned earlier, namely Proposition
4.6
.

2.

Verify a tail bound for the spherical model with reduced mass, and boundary condition from the gradient estimates of the separation. The tail bound is for the spherical law with a natural harmonic extension (with correctly chosen mass) removed. The subtlety here is that the boundary condition can be large, but not
O(N)
and we want the tail bound even near the boundary. This is done in
Lemma

3.3
and is of independent interest.

3.

Use the tail bound to upgrade the
\ell^{\infty}
bound to be
o(N^{\beta})
on the outside of an expanded set
\tilde{U}
, which none the less has poly-logarithmic size, and moreover, the mass fraction on
\tilde{U}
is still close to the mass fraction on
U
. Note here that even a polylog size set outside
U
can have potentially a lot of mass as the
\ell^{\infty}
-bound is only
\sqrt{\varepsilon N}
. We bypass this by finding set containing
U
using a delicate choice of parameters from the tempering
Proposition

4.6
. This is done in
Proposition

5.6
.

4.

Use the subcritical argument, namely that the non linear exponential tilt is now
o(1)
on
\tilde{U}^{c}
, to show that the local limit on
\tilde{U}^{c}
is the same as the spherical model with small boundary condition, which is separately proven to locally converge to a massive GFF. Technicalities one needs to overcome involve preserving some aspects of domain Markov property, despite the global
\ell^{\infty}
-bound imposed by conditioning on
U
.

All of the above ideas are executed in
Section

5.2
.

We now turn to Theorem
1.2
.

1.

Re-express the spherical law in the Fourier Basis. This makes it clear that we can regard the spherical law as the sum of a zero average GFF and a random constant that is uniform on the disk of radius
\sqrt{N}
in
\mathbb{C}
, conditioned to have
\ell^{2}
norm appropriately bounded above.

2.

The Fourier basis is also useful, as we may represent
\left\|\Phi_{m}\right\|_{2}^{2}
and
\left\|\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
as the sum of independent exponential random variables.

3.

For
\mathfrak{g}:\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}\to\mathbb{C}
, an arbitrary function supported on a fixed and finite set
A\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
, we express
\E|\langle\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N},\mathfrak{g}\rangle|^{2r}
for some
r>0
in terms of expectations of the random variables in the previous point, obtaining a sum of several ratios of expectations, involving monomials of fixed finite degrees.

4.

In the case
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
, the conditioning is not too atypical, and is actually typical when
\theta>C_{d}
. We use the convergence in probability of
N^{-1}\cdot\left\|\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, and the fact that the limit is the same when a fixed finite number of exponential random variables constituting the sum
\left\|\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
are removed. For the case
\theta=C_{d}
, an anti-concentration estimate is additionally used to bound the denominator term from below. The subtlety here is that some of the exponential random variables constituting
\left\|\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
have diverging mean as
N\to\infty
, this requires some care, especially when
d=3
.

5.

For the case
\theta<C_{d}
introduce an exponential tilt by
\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{2}^{2}
to make the conditioning typical. Controlling the ratio of expectations requires a refined version of the Lindeberg-Feller central limit theorem. The subtlety here arises in considering the case with boundary conditions, and the contribution of a massive harmonic function needs to be carefully canceled out.

1.4
Summary of notations

The following notation is standardized and appears throughout the article.

1.

We use standard big
O
and little
o
notation. To recall, let
a_{N}
and
b_{N}
be sequences, such that
a_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
. Then

(a)

b_{N}
is
O(a_{N})
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
b_{N}\leqslant Ca_{N}
.

(b)

b_{N}
is
o(a_{N})
if
b_{N}/a_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

(c)

b_{N}\asymp a_{N}
if there exist
0<c<C
such that
cb_{N}\leqslant a_{N}\leqslant Cb_{N}
. We also denote this as
b_{N}=\Omega(a_{N})
.

2.

We will denote by
d(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
the graph distance between
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
. This makes its first appearance in Section
2.1
. Also, for
B\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
,

d(\boldsymbol{x},B):=\min\{d(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}):\boldsymbol{y}\in B\}.

3.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
. Then
\partial B
denotes the inner boundary of
B
, that is

\partial B:=\{\boldsymbol{x}:\boldsymbol{x}\in B\text{ and }d(\boldsymbol{x},B^{c})=1\}.

and
\bar{B}=B\cup\partial B^{c}
. (defined in
Section

2.1
)

4.

Let
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function, and
B\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
. Then we denote the restriction of
f
to
B
as
f|_{B}
, that is

f|_{B}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\begin{cases}f(\boldsymbol{x})\text{ when }x\in B,\\
0\text{ when }\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}.\end{cases}

This makes its first appearance in Section
2.1
.

5.

All norms, unless explicitly mentioned are for functions
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}\to\mathbb{C}
, and the relevant ones are

\left\|f\right\|_{p}^{p}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{p},\text{ }\left\|\nabla f\right\|_{2}^{2}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})-f(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}.

6.

The following random fields are always denoted as indicated:

(a)

\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}
denotes the focusing NLS field, at temperature
\theta^{-1}
and strength of non linearity
\nu
. (defined in
Section

1
)

(b)

\Phi_{m}
,
\Phi^{U}_{m}
and
\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}
denote the various avatars of the Gaussian Free Field; the massive, massive with Dirichlet boundary conditions and zero average respectively. These are defined in
Section

2

(c)

\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}
denotes the usual spherical law at inverse temperature
\theta
defined in
Section

1
, and
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}
denotes the spherical law on
U^{c}
with boundary condition
f
on
U
, defined in
section

3
.

7.

The following constants are important and are always denoted as such:

(a)

C_{d}={\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})
, the diagonal of the Green’s function on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, closely related to the expected number of returns of the simple symmetric random walk.

2
GFF preliminaries

The overarching reference for this section is Chapter 8 of
[
14
]
, and specific proofs are provided for results that are not entirely standard but are required for this article. Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite, connected graph and recall the Laplacian operator as defined in (
1.9
) acting on
\mathbb{C}^{V}
. It is a negative semidefinite matrix acting on
\mathds{C}^{V}
, and has kernel of dimension 1 which is spanned by the constant functions on
G
. Let
B\subset V
. We also need to work with the Dirichlet Laplacian
restricted on
B^{c}
as follows. For any function
f:V\to\mathbb{C}
, recall that
f|_{B^{c}}\equiv f
on
B^{c}
and 0 on
B
. Then
\Delta_{B^{c}}f=g
where
g(\boldsymbol{x})=\Delta_{B^{c}}f|_{B^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x})
for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}
and
g(\boldsymbol{x})=0
for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in B
. This definition may seem backward, but it is important from our perspective, as we want to think of the set
B
as relatively small, and where we may impose boundary conditions.
The inverse and resolvents of
\Delta_{B^{c}}
play a prominent role in this work, and we provide a probabilistic description of them now. Let
\sf D_{B^{c}}
denote the diagonal matrix with entries given by the degrees of the vertices of
B^{c}
. Note that for a given
B\subset V
and
m\geqslant 0
,
(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)
is positive definite when either
B^{c}\neq\emptyset
or
m>0
. It is also useful to recall that

(2.1)

({\sf D}_{B^{c}})^{-1}(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)=-P_{B^{c}}+I+m{\sf D}_{B^{c}}^{-1},

where
P_{B^{c}}
is the sub-probability kernel for the simple random walk on
B^{c}
, killed when it enters
B
(sub-probability as the walk has positive probability of entering
B
). This gives us a relation for the inverse of
(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)
in terms of the kernel of a killed random walk. Specifically, when we rearrange,

(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)={\sf D}_{B^{c}}(I+m{\sf D}_{B^{c}}^{-1})(I-(1+m{\sf D}_{B^{c}}^{-1})^{-1}P_{B^{c}}).

Consider a random walk on
G
, denoted
X_{t}^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}
, started at
\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}
which is killed with probability
\frac{m}{m+\deg(\boldsymbol{y})}
when it is at
\boldsymbol{y}\in B^{c}
, and is also killed when it enters
B
. It is not hard to verify that

(I-(1+m{\sf D}_{B^{c}}^{-1})^{-1}P_{B^{c}})^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{y}),

which is the expected number of times that
X_{t}
visits
y
before being killed. All assembled, we obtain

(2.2)

\displaystyle{\sf G}^{m,B^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}):=(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X_{t}^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{y})\cdot({\sf D}_{B^{c}}+m)^{-1},

where
{\sf G}^{m,B^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
is called the
massive Green’s function
with mass
m
and Dirichlet boundary condition at
B
. In our case, where
B,B^{c}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
,
{\sf D}_{B^{c}}
is replaced by
2dI
.

2.1
Massive GFF with Dirichlet boundary condition

A
complex Gaussian
with variance
\sigma^{2}
is a random variable
X+iY
where
X,Y\sim
i.i.d. N
(0,\sigma^{2}/2)
. A standard complex Gaussian is a complex Gaussian with variance 1. Complex Gaussians have a useful property, if
X
is complex gaussian with variance
\sigma^{2}
, then
|X|^{2}
is exponential with mean
\sigma^{2}
. We assume
G=(V,E)
is a finite, connected, simple graph in this subsection.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
B\subseteq V
and
m\geqslant 0
. Assume
m=0
only if
B\neq\emptyset
.
The complex valued
massive Gaussian Free Field on
G
=
(V,E)
with
Dirichlet boundary condition on
B
and mass
m
is a centered Gaussian process
\{\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})\}_{\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}}
on the vertices of
V
which is identically 0 on
B
and

\mathbb{E}(\overline{\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})}\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{y}))={\sf G}^{m,B^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}),\qquad\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in B.

where
{\sf G}^{m,B^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
is as defined in (
2.2
).

Equivalently, if
\{\lambda_{B^{c},k}\}_{k=1}^{|B^{c}|}
and
\{\phi_{B^{c},k}\}_{k=1}^{|B^{c}|}
respectively denote the eigenvalues and orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of
\Delta_{B^{c}}
, then we may define the massive Gaussian free field as

\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{k=1}^{|B^{c}|}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{B,k}+m}}\phi_{B,k}(\boldsymbol{x})

where
\{X_{k}\}_{k=1}^{|V|}
is a family of i.i.d. standard complex Gaussian random variables.

The relation to this work stems from the form of the density of
\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}
as a random vector taking values in
\mathbb{C}^{B^{c}}
, it can easily be verified that for any
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{B^{c}}\to\mathbb{C}
,

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}))=\frac{\det(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)}{\pi^{|B^{c}|}}\int\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\cdot\exp(-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}-m\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2})\mathbf{d}\psi_{B^{c}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\psi|_{\partial B}=0}.

where
\partial B
denotes the inner boundary, that is vertices in
B
that neighbour vertices outside
B
.
In the specific context when
B=\emptyset
and
m>0
, we replace
\Phi^{\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}_{m}
by
\Phi^{N}_{m}
for brevity. Moreover, we have explicit expressions for the eigenvalues and eigenvectors. We label vertices in
\mathds{T}^{d}_{n}
as
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})
, where
x_{i}\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
. The eigenvalues and eigenvectors are indexed by a copy of the same torus (corresponding to the Pontryagin dual). We will index them by
\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots k_{d})
. We have that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{d}}}\exp\left({i\mkern 1.0mu}\frac{2\pi}{n}\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}\right),

and

(2.4)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}=4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{i}}{n}\right).

We now record a formula for
\mathbb{E}(\|\Phi^{N}_{m}\|_{2}^{2})
which is a simple consequence of
2.1
, Parseval’s identity, the explicit formulas (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and the Riemann sum formula:

(2.5)

\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}(\|\Phi^{N}_{m}\|^{2}_{2})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}{\sf G}^{m}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})={\sf G}^{m}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}+m}\\
=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\kappa}}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}(\pi\kappa_{i})+m}+o_{N}(1).

where
o_{N}(1)
is a function which tends to 0 as
N\to\infty
.
We now move towards describing the GFF with prescribed boundary conditions, as well as the extremely useful domain Markov property. An important ingredient here is the notion of harmonic functions. Given
B\subset V
, let recall that
\partial B
denotes the inner boundary, that is vertices in
B
that neighbour vertices outside
B
.

Definition 2.2
.

In particular, given
B\subset V
and
f:\partial B\to\mathbb{C}
, we say a complex valued function
h^{B^{c},f}
is massive harmonic on
B^{c}
with boundary condition
f
if

((-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)h^{B^{c},f})(\boldsymbol{x})=0\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}\text{ and }h^{B^{c},f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(\boldsymbol{x})\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial B.

Observe that if
h^{B^{c},f}
is complex valued and massive harmonic then both its real and imaginary parts are separately massive harmonic with the same mass. It is a standard fact that the massive harmonic function exists and is unique. We now provide the extremely convenient and standard description in terms of the killed random walk, which also makes an appearance in the expression for the Green’s function. Let
\{X_{t}^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}
denote a simple symmetric random walk started at a point
\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}
that is killed with probability
\frac{m}{m+2d}
at each step, and is also killed when it enters
B
, which will clearly be at a boundary vertex in
\partial B
. Let

The massive harmonic function with boundary values
f
may be represented as

(2.6)

\displaystyle h^{B,f}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})=\mathbb{E}(f({X^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}_{T_{m,B}}})).

The convention here is that the boundary value when killed is taken to be
0
, that is

. Expanded,

(2.7)

\displaystyle h^{B^{c},f}_{m}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial B}f(\boldsymbol{y})\cdot\mathds{P}(X^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}_{T_{m,B}}=\boldsymbol{y}).

Definition 2.3
.

We define the massive GFF on
B^{c}
with prescribed boundary values given by
f:\partial B\to\mathds{C}
, denoted
\Phi_{m}^{B^{c},f}
as

(2.8)

\displaystyle\Phi^{B^{c},f}_{m}=\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}+h^{B^{c},f}_{m}

In terms of density, it can easily be verified using summation by parts that

\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Phi_{m}^{B^{c},f})=\frac{\det(-\Delta_{B^{c}}+m)}{\pi^{|B^{c}|}}\int\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\cdot\exp(-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}-m\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2})\mathbf{d}\psi_{B^{c}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\psi|_{\partial B}=f}.

We now state the well known
Domain Markov Property
of the massive Gaussian free field. For any graph
G=(V,E)
and
B\subset V
, we can decompose

(2.9)

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}=\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}+h^{B^{c}}

where
\Phi^{B}_{m}
is a massive GFF on with Dirichlet boundary condition on
B
and
h^{B^{c}}
is massive harmonic on
B^{c}
, and equal to
\Phi_{m}
on
B
, in particular with boundary values
\Phi_{m}|_{\partial B}
. Furthermore,
\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}
and
h^{B^{c}}
are independent random fields (where we think of
\Phi^{B^{c}}_{m}
as a field on
G
with values 0 on
B^{c}
).

The killing rate of the random walk in terms of which the massive harmonic function is expressed leads to some important consequences.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
be fixed, and
f:\partial B\to\mathds{C}
. Let
h
be the massive harmonic extension of
f
onto
B^{c}
with mass
m>0
. Then there exists a constant
c
depending only on
m
such that for every
\boldsymbol{x}
,

|h(\boldsymbol{x})|\leqslant\|f|_{\partial B}\|_{1}e^{-cd(\boldsymbol{x},\partial B)}.

There also exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
m
such that

\left\|h|_{B}\right\|^{p}_{p}\leqslant C\left\|f|_{\partial B}\right\|^{p}_{p}

for all
p>1

Proof.

We will use the killed random walk description of massive harmonic function (
2.6
).
Since the killing probability in every step is
\frac{m}{m+2d}
, we must have

\mathds{P}(X_{T_{m,B}}^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{y})\leqslant\left(\frac{m}{m+2d}\right)^{d(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})}.

The first item is now immediate.
For the second item, note that

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial B}\mathbb{P}(X^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}_{T_{m,B}}=\boldsymbol{y})f(\boldsymbol{y})\right)^{p}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial B}\mathbb{P}(X^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}_{T_{m.B}}=\boldsymbol{y})|f(\boldsymbol{y})|^{p}.

where the last inequality follows from an application of Jensen’s inequality. Next, we may swap the order of summation to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{p}^{p}\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial B}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in B}\mathbb{P}(X^{m,\boldsymbol{x}}_{T_{m.B}}=\boldsymbol{y})|f(\boldsymbol{y})|^{p}\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial B}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in B^{c}}e^{-cd(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})}|f(\boldsymbol{y})|^{p}\leqslant C\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}\in\partial B}|f(\boldsymbol{y})|^{p}.

where
c=c(m)
and
C=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{-cd(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})}
.

\blacksquare

Now we prove a lemma stating that slightly perturbing the torus does not change the formula (
2.5
). Essentially this is a consequence of the fact that far enough away from the perturbation, the Dirichlet and full torus massive GFFs are close in distribution, thanks to the domain Markov property and decay of harmonic functions.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
m>0
and
U\subset\mathds{T}^{d}_{n}
be such that
|U|=O((\log N)^{10d})
. Let
\{\lambda_{U^{c},k}\}_{k=1}^{|U^{c}|}
be as in
2.1
. Then

\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}(\|\Phi^{U^{c}}_{m}\|^{2}_{2})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}{\sf G}^{m,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{|U^{c}|}\frac{1}{\lambda_{U,k}+m}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\kappa}}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}(\pi\kappa_{i})+m}+o_{N}(1).

Proof.

There are many methods of proof for this statement, we find the proof using the Domain Markov Property of the massive Gaussian Free field to be the easiest. Starting with
\Phi_{m}
, Let
\Phi_{m}^{U^{c}}
be an independent Dirichlet MGFF which is identically
0
on
U
, and let
h_{m}^{U^{c}}
be equal to
\Phi_{m}
on
U
, and massive harmonic on
U^{c}
, and they are all related as per (
2.9
). Now, note that by independence,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in U^{c}}{\sf G}^{m,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})=\E\left\|\Phi_{m}^{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\E\left\|\Phi_{m}|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\E\left\|h|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

By definition,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}}{\sf G}^{m}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})=\E\left\|\Phi_{m}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\E\left\|\Phi_{m}|_{U^{c}}\right\|^{2}_{2}+\E\left\|\Phi_{m}|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

By translation invariance, for any
A\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\Phi_{m}|_{A}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in A}|\Phi_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}=C(m)|A|.

By combining Lemma
2.4
and (
2.12
),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left\|h|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant C|U|.

Finally, subtracting (
2.10
) from (
2.11
), and bringing in the bounds from (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) yields

|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in U^{c}}{\sf G}^{m,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})-{\sf G}^{m}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})|=O(\log^{11d}(N)/N)=o_{N}(1).

\blacksquare

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
U\subset\mathds{T}^{d}_{n}
be such that
|U|=O((\log N)^{10d})
.
\{\lambda_{U^{c},k}\}_{k=1}^{|U^{c}|}
be as in
2.1
. Then

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{|U^{c}|}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{U^{c},k}+m)^{2}}=\Omega(1)

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
, we know that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in U^{c}}{\sf G}^{m,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{|U^{c}|}\frac{1}{\lambda_{U^{c},k}+m}\to\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\kappa}}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}(\pi\kappa_{i})+m}.

We may interpret the sum as the integral of the function
(x+m)^{-1}
against the empirical spectral measure of the Dirichlet Laplacian, given by

\rho_{\Delta,U^{c}}=\frac{1}{|U^{c}|}\sum_{k=1}^{|U^{c}|}\delta_{\lambda_{U^{c},k}}.

With
\rho_{\Delta,U^{c}}
defined as above,

\frac{1}{N}{\sf G}^{m,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{|U^{c}|}{N}\int\frac{1}{x+m}d\rho_{\Delta,m}(x).

The intergal above is the Stieltjes transform of
\rho_{\Delta,m}
, and the convergence of the Stieltjes transform to a limit guarantees the weak convergence of
\rho_{\Delta,m}
to a limiting probability measure
\rho_{*}
, since our measures are compactly supported which guarantees tightness (see Chapter 2 of
[
22
]
). The convergence of the Stieltjes transforms holds since
|U^{c}|/N\to 1
. Next, note that the function
(x+m)^{-2}
is bounded and continuous on the interval
[0,4d]
. Thus,

\frac{1}{|U^{c}|}\sum_{k=1}^{|U|^{c}}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{U^{c},k}+m)^{2}}=\int\frac{d\rho_{\Delta,U^{c}}}{(x+m)^{2}}\to\int\frac{d\rho_{*}}{(x+m)^{2}}\text{ as }N\to\infty.

which completes the proof.

\blacksquare

2.2
Zero average GFF on the torus

In this subsection, we consider the case
G=\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
and
m=0
. Note that in this case
(-\Delta)
is not positive definite and has an eigenvalue 0 corresponding the eigenvector which is constant on the torus. Thus to define the Green’s function, we need to project onto the orthogonal complement of the constant vector.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
(\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}
and
(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}
be the orthonormal basis of eigenvectors and their eigenvalues as specified in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
). Then the
zero-average
GFF on the torus is defined as

\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\frac{X_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}}}\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}}

The zero-average Green’s function is defined to be the covariance of the zero-average GFF:

\mathsf{G}_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\mathbb{E}(\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{y})}).

It is not too hard to see that the density of a zero-average GFF is simply a GFF conditioned to have sum 0. Or in other words, for any bounded continuous function
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(2.14)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}))=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{0\text{-}\mathsf{avg}}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\exp(-\|\nabla\psi\|_{2}^{2}1_{\langle\psi,\boldsymbol{1}\rangle=0})

where
\boldsymbol{1}
is the function which is identically equal to 1 on the torus. One of the first investigations into the properties of the zero average free field was carried out by Abächerli in
[
1
]
, who established an extremely useful coupling between
\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}
and its infinite volume counterpart to be introduced in the next subsection.

2.3
Infinite volume GFF

We assume
d\geqslant 2,m\geqslant 0
and furthermore, we need to assume
m>0
only if
d=2
. The massive Green’s function, as defined in (
2.2
), is still a well defined object when the killed random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is used instead. In fact, when
d\geqslant 3
, we may use the simple random walk with no killing, since it is transient and thus the sum in (
2.2
) is finite almost surely. Thus,
{\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{m}
is well defined as a positive definite kernel, as is
{\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. The centered Gaussian processes with covariance given by these kernels, denoted
\Phi_{m}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
are called the infinite volume massive GFF and infinite volume GFF respectively. To summarize, they are defined by

\mathbb{E}(\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{y})})={\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}).

Moreover,
\Phi_{m}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is the local limit of its torus counterpart. It is well known that the limit as
N\to\infty
of
{\sf G}^{N}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
is
{\sf G}_{m}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, which by Wick’s theorem and the method of moments is sufficient to verify local convergence. The massless GFF also arises as a local limit, but as discussed earlier, we cannot immediately impose the condition that
m=0
, we need to work with the zero average Green’s function.

Lemma 2.8

(Aru, Korzhenkova Proposition 2.13
[
2
]
)
.

As
N\to\infty
, there exists
C>0
such that for any chosen lattice point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
,

|\mathsf{G}_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{x})-{\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{x})|\leqslant CN^{\frac{2}{d}-1}.

We emphasize the crucial point in
Lemma

2.8
, the constant
C
is independent of
\boldsymbol{x}
.
The convergence of the finite volume Green’s function to its infinite volume counterpart has been known for some time, but the rate of convergence in Lemma
2.8
was put forth by
[
2
]
. Let
\mathfrak{g}:A\to\mathbb{C}
. Note that for all
N
,
\langle\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N},\mathfrak{g}\rangle
is a complex gaussian random variable with variance given by
\langle\mathfrak{g},\mathsf{G}_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}\mathfrak{g}\rangle
. Since all moments of a gaussian are determined by the variance, proving that
\mathsf{G}_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}
converges to
{\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
verifies the convergence in distribution, and in particular that

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathcal{P}(\langle\mathfrak{g},\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}\rangle)\bigr)-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathcal{P}(\langle\mathfrak{g},\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\rangle)\bigr)\right|\to 0\text{ as }N\to\infty,

for all
\mathfrak{g}:A\to\mathbb{C}
and
\mathcal{P}(z)
of the form
(\mathfrak{R}z)^{q}\cdot(\mathfrak{I}z)^{q^{\prime}}
, with
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
. With the exception of the sharpened Green’s function estimates of Lemma
2.8
, the other results mentioned in this section are well known in the literature and we again direct the reader to Chapter 8 of
[
14
]
for reference.

3
Further estimates on the spherical model

In this section, we gather some results on the spherical model which are needed to prove
1.1
. These results are proven in
Section

6
. The reader should recall the definition of
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}
from (
1.4
).
First, the
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
case. Along with the second item of
1.2
we also need the following uniform tail bound.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
and
b>\log N
. Then for
N
sufficiently large, there exists a constant
c
such that

\mathbb{P}(\left\|\Psi_{{\sf sph},N}^{\theta}\right\|_{\infty}>b)\leqslant e^{-cb^{2}}.

The key point in
Lemma

3.1
is that
b
might diverge with
N
, and hence not captured by the local limit identification of
1.2
. The proof of
Lemma

3.1
is postponed to
Section

6
.

Next, the
\theta<C_{d}
case. In the proof of
1.1
, we need the following generalization of the first item of
1.2
for the spherical law with boundary conditions. Let
U
be a subset of the set of vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
,
f:U\to\mathbb{C}
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
The spherical law with general boundary condition and general mass cutoff is the field
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c}}
whose law is dictated by the following equality for all bounded continous functions
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}}\to\mathbb{R}
.

(3.1)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c}})):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c})}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}}}\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\exp\left(-\theta\left\|\nabla\psi|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\gamma N}1_{\psi|_{\partial U}\equiv f_{|\partial U}}\mathbf{d}\psi.

The gradient above is calculated in the graph
G_{U}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c})
is the appropriate partition function. If
\gamma=1
, we write
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,1,U^{c}}=\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}
. Also note the scaling relation

(3.2)

\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c}}=\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta\gamma,U^{c}}.

Recall the definition of local weak convergence from
Section

1
and the definition of massive GFF in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
from
Section

2

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose
\theta<C_{d}
. Let
U\subset\mathds{T}^{d}_{n}
be such that
d(\boldsymbol{0},U)\geqslant N^{0.1}
,
|U|=O((\log N)^{10d})
, and
\|f|_{\partial U}\|_{2}^{2}=o(N)
. Then

\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{(d)}\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{m}

where the convergence above is in the local weak sense and
m
solves

(3.3)

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\kappa}}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}(\pi\kappa_{i})+m}=\theta.

As mentioned in the introduction, this result generalizes the results in Aru and Korzhenkova
[
2
]
for the massive phase. In terms of the boundary condition, we believe this result is sharp as we can expect a loss of mass in the local limit if, for example,
U
is a singleton with boundary value
\sqrt{N}
.

We also need the following uniform bound analogous to the
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
case. For the application, we need a bound with more general boundary conditions, which might diverge with
N
. Recall that
\Delta_{U}
is the restriction of the Laplacian to
U
as defined in
Section

2
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Fix
\theta<C_{d}
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
and let
f:U\to\mathbb{C}
be such that
\|f|_{\partial U}\|_{2}^{2}=o(N)
and
|U|=O((\log N)^{100d})
. Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{|U^{c}|}
denote the eigenvalues corresponding to an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of
-\Delta_{U^{c}}
. Let
h
denote the massive harmonic extension of
f
onto
U^{c}
with mass
m
given by (
3.3
). Let
m
be defined as

(3.4)

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}+m}+\theta\|h|_{U^{c}}\|_{2}^{2}=\theta N

Let
h^{m_{N},f}
denote the massive harmonic extension of
f
with mass given by (
3.4
).
Then there exists a constant
c,C>0
such that for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in U^{c}
and all
b\geqslant 2|h(\boldsymbol{y})|
,

\mathbb{P}(|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{y})-h^{m,N,f}(\boldsymbol{y})|>b)\leqslant\exp(-cb^{2}).

The proof if postponed of there results is postponed to Section
6
, along with that of
1.2
.

4
Local limit of focusing NLS

4.1
Tempering the NLS field

The goal of this section is to prove
Proposition

4.6
which roughly states that the NLS field cannot take
O(\sqrt{N})
values outside a small set of polylogarithmic order. This result is general and works for all
p>2
and all
\nu,\theta>0
. The amount of mass that is concentrated in this small set depends on whether
\nu<\nu_{c}(\theta)
or
\nu>\nu_{c}(\theta)
.

We start two quick lemmas which we shall use multiple times in this article.

Lemma 4.1
.

Fix
p\geqslant 2
.
Let
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
be such that
\|f\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant a
and
\|f\|_{\infty}<b
. Then

\|f\|_{p}^{p}\leqslant b^{p-2}a.

Proof.

The largest possible
\ell^{p}
norm is achieved when the function
f
takes its largest value on as many lattice sites as possible. In other words,
|f|=b
on
a/b^{2}
many lattice sites and
0
everwhere else. This is a consequence of the following inequality, for all
s>t>0
and
p>2
we have that

s^{p/2}+t^{p/2}\leqslant(s+\alpha)^{p/2}+(t-\alpha)^{p/2}

for all
\alpha\in[0,t]
(which can be checked by taking the derivative with respect to
\alpha
.)
To see how to use this inequality, start from a configuration with equal mass at every site (satisfying the
\ell^{2}
-constraint.) Then try to pile up as much mass onto one site from other sites given the
\ell^{\infty}
-constraint. The
\ell^{p}
-norm only increases for this operation. The details are left to the reader.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.2
.

Fix
p\geqslant 2
.
Let
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\left\|f\right\|^{2}_{2}=a

\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}=b
, and
b\leqslant\sqrt{a}
. Then

\left\|f\right\|_{p}^{p}\geqslant b^{p}+(a-b^{2})^{p/2}\cdot(|A|-1)^{-(p-2)/2}.

Proof.

There has to be atleast one
\boldsymbol{x}\in A
such that
|f_{\boldsymbol{x}}|=b
, which contributes
b^{p}
to
\left\|f\right\|_{p}^{p}
. We have mass
a-b^{2}
which remains to be distributed across the remainder of the lattice sites. The configuration that yields the lowest
\ell^{p}
norm is thus realised by evenly distributing the remainder of the mass across the other lattice sites. This fact has a proof analogous to
Lemma

4.1
.

\blacksquare

The following corollary will be useful later in controlling the exponential tilt by the
\ell^{p}
norm:

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
p>4
and
0<\beta<\frac{(p-2)-2}{2(p-2)}
. Then for all
\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n})
satisfying
\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N
and
\left\|\psi\right\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta}
,

\frac{1}{N^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}\to 0,

as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
, we know that for such
\psi
,

\frac{1}{N^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}\leqslant\frac{1}{N^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}\cdot N^{\beta(p-2)+1}=N^{(p-2)\beta+(1-\frac{p-2}{2})}.

For our choice of
\beta,p
, the exponent is negative. This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Controlling the tilt requires the following notion introduced in
[
13
]
. Given a function
\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n})
with
\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}<N
and
\varepsilon>0
, we define the
separating set

U(\psi,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
to be the set obtained via the following procedure: Let
U_{0}=\{\boldsymbol{x}:|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{2}\geqslant\varepsilon N\}
. Clearly
|U_{0}|\leqslant 1/\varepsilon
. We inductively define
U_{i}
by the successive addition of the 2-step outer boundary,
i.e.,

U_{i}=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in V:d(\boldsymbol{x},U_{i-1})\leqslant 2\}
and
B_{i}=U_{i}\setminus U_{i-1}
for all
i\geqslant 1
. Take
B_{0}=U_{0}
. Note for any
k\geqslant 1
,

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{x}\in B_{i}\end{subarray}}|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{2}\leqslant\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{2}\leqslant N.

By pigeonhole principle, there must be an
i\in\{1,\ldots,\lceil\frac{10}{\varepsilon}\rceil\}
such that
\|\psi|_{B_{i}}\|^{2}_{2}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{10}N
. Let
i_{0}
be the smallest such
i
and define
U=U(\psi,\varepsilon)=U_{i_{0}-1}\cup\{\boldsymbol{x}:d(\boldsymbol{x},U_{i_{0}-1})=1\}
.

Note that

|U|\leqslant(2\lceil\frac{10}{\varepsilon}\rceil+1)^{d}|U_{0}|\leqslant(21)^{d}\varepsilon^{-d-1}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-d-2}

if
\varepsilon<1/(21)^{d}
(which we assume from now on).
We compile some important properties of the separating set below, the proofs are almost immediate consequences of the definitions.

Lemma 4.4
.

A separating set set
U(\psi,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
satisfies the following properties:

i.

|U|\leqslant\varepsilon^{-d-2},

ii.

|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{2}<\varepsilon N\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{x}\notin U,

iii.

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in B_{i_{0}}}|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{2}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in U_{\text{int}}\cup U_{\text{out}}}|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{2}<\frac{\varepsilon N}{10},
where
U_{\text{int}}
and
U_{\text{out}}
are the inner and outer shells of
B_{i_{0}}
. To be precise,
U_{\text{int}}=\{\boldsymbol{x}\not\in U_{i_{0}-1}:d(\boldsymbol{x},U_{i_{0}-1})=1\}
and
U_{\text{out}}=B_{i_{0}}\setminus U_{\text{int}}
.

The discussion before the definition guarantees the existence of the separating set.
The last point in the
Lemma

4.4
is important because it implies that the gradient of
\psi
on the boundary of
U
can be controlled.

Now, recall our protagonist
\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}
, the focusing NLS field with parameters
\nu
and
\theta
from (
1.2
). It is worthwhile to spend some time describing the conditional law of the NLS field given the separating set and the field values on it.
For some
A\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
and
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
, let
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}(A,f,\varepsilon)
be the following event:

•

U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\varepsilon_{N})=A

•

\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{A}\equiv f
.

Let
\alpha=\frac{1}{N}\|f|_{A}\|_{2}^{2}
. Let
\Psi_{N,\varepsilon}^{\nu,\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}
denote the random field distributed as
\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}
in
A^{c}
conditioned on the event
\mathcal{U}(A,f,\varepsilon)
.

Lemma 4.5
.

The random field
\Psi_{N,\varepsilon}^{\nu,\theta,1-\alpha,f,A}
, has a density given by

(4.1)

\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\prime}}\exp\left[\theta\left(\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi|_{A^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant(1-\alpha)N}\boldsymbol{1}_{\|\psi|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon N}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\psi|_{A}\equiv f|_{A}},\qquad\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{\bar{A}^{c}}

for an appropriate partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\prime}(\nu,\theta,1-\alpha,f,A,\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

This simply follows from the fact that we chose the first
i_{0}
while defining the separating set, which preserves the Markov property. The remaining details are straightforward consequence of the density of the NLS measure.

\blacksquare

Proposition 4.6
.

Fix
\nu,\theta>0
and
p>2
.
Let
\varepsilon_{N}\in(e^{-\sqrt{\log N}},1)
with
\varepsilon_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, and let
U(\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N},\varepsilon_{N})
be the separating set as described above.
Then there exists
\delta_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{N}\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{U}\|^{2}_{2}\in(a-\delta_{N},a+\delta_{N})\text{ for some }a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)\right)\geqslant 1-e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}

In particular, if
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)=\{0\}
then

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}\|_{\infty}<\sqrt{\max\{\varepsilon_{N},\delta_{N}\}N}\right)\geqslant 1-e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}.

Proof.

The second assertion immediately follows from the first and the definition of the separating set, so we concentrate on the first assertion.
In this proof we drop superscript
\nu,\theta
from
\Psi^{\nu,\theta}
for the sake of brevity. Recall the notation of the energy functional
F
from (
1.12
).
Let

\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N}):=\{x\in[0,1),F(x)-F_{\min}<\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}\}.

We claim that

(4.2)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{N}\left\|\Psi_{N}|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}\notin\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})\right)\leqslant e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}

for
N
sufficiently large. This bound follows from the upper and lower bounds of the partition function from
[
13
]
*Section 6. We now provide a sketch of the proof of these bounds (
4.2
) for the sake of completeness, before completing the proof. Let us define

\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}):=\{\psi:\frac{1}{N}\left\|\psi|_{U(\psi,\varepsilon_{N})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\notin\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})\}.

Note,

\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\int_{\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})\cap\{\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N\}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi)}\mathbf{d}\psi.

For a given
A\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
, whose size is bounded above by
\varepsilon^{-d-2}_{N}
, let
\mathcal{C}(A,\varepsilon_{N})
denote the collection of
\psi
such that
A=U(\psi,\varepsilon_{N})
. We can now split
\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi)
as

\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi)=\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A})+\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A^{c}})+\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\sim\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{x}\in A,\boldsymbol{y}\in A^{c}}|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\psi_{\boldsymbol{y}}|^{2}

Using property iii. of
U
above, the inequality
|a-b|^{2}\leqslant 2(|a|^{2}+|b|^{2})
and employing an union bound over all possible separating sets of size at most
\varepsilon_{N}^{-d-2}
, we obtain the following upper bound

\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}))\leqslant\frac{e^{\theta 3N\varepsilon_{N}}}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\sum_{A:|A|\leqslant\varepsilon_{N}^{-d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})\cap\{\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N\}\cap\mathcal{C}(A,\varepsilon_{N})}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A})-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A^{c}})}\mathbf{d}\psi

With
\xi:=1-\frac{1}{2d}
(this choice is governed by the error bounds from the entropy terms and follows
[
13
, Section 6]
), define

\mathcal{B}_{i,A}=\left\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{A}:\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2}\in\bigl[iN^{\xi},(i+1)N^{\xi}\bigr)\right\},

and

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{j,A^{c}}:=\left\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{A^{c}}:\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2}\in(jN^{\xi},(j+1)N^{\xi}),\text{ }\left\|\zeta\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}\text{ and }\left\|\zeta|_{\partial(A^{c})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant 3\varepsilon_{N}N\right\}.

By definition of
\mathcal{C}(A,\varepsilon_{N})
and
\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})
, the following containment holds:

\{\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N\}\cap\mathcal{C}(A,\varepsilon_{N})\cap\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})\subseteq\bigcup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i+j\leqslant N^{1-\xi}}}{{iN^{\xi-1}\not\in\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})}}}\mathcal{B}_{i,A}\times\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{j,A^{c}}.

This yields

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}))\leqslant\frac{e^{3N\varepsilon_{N}\theta}}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\sum_{A:|A|\leqslant\varepsilon^{-d-2}}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i+j\leqslant N^{1-\xi}}}{{iN^{\xi-1}\not\in\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})}}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,A}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A})}\mathbf{d}\psi|_{A}\cdot\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{j,A^{c}}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A^{c}})}\mathbf{d}\psi|_{A^{c}}

It is a consequence of Lemmas 6.4, 6.5 and the proof of Lemma 6.2 of
[
13
]
that we can further bound (
4.3
) above by

(4.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\cdot e^{10N\varepsilon_{N}+o(N\varepsilon_{N})}\cdot\left(\frac{\pi}{\theta}\right)^{N}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=0}}{{iN^{\xi-1}\not\in\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})}}}^{i^{*}}e^{-NF(i\cdot N^{\xi})}

where
i^{*}=\lceil N^{1-\xi}\rceil
. Specifically, Lemma 6.4 addresses the intergral of
\psi|_{A}
, Lemma 6.5 addresses the integral over
\psi|_{A^{c}}
, and the proof of Lemma 6.2 demonstrates how to combine the respective bounds so as to make the saddle point method applicable. For ease of reference, we mention the differences in notation now,
\varepsilon_{N}
here is
s_{N}
in
[
13
]
, and
N^{\xi}
here is replaced by a general sequence
\kappa_{N}
in
[
13
]
, where an exact rate is specified later.
By definition, we have that

F(a)>F_{\min}+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}\text{ for all }a\notin\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N}).

Thus, a further upper bound of (
4.4
) is obtained as

\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}e^{10N\varepsilon_{N}+o(N\varepsilon_{N})}\cdot N^{1-\xi}\left(\frac{\pi}{\theta}\right)^{N}\cdot e^{-NF_{\min}}\cdot e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}}

To extract the required upper bound on
\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}))
, the last part required is the lower bound on
\mathcal{Z}_{N}
, which by Lemma 6.6 of
[
13
]
, for
N
sufficiently large is given by

\mathcal{Z}_{N}\geqslant e^{o(N\varepsilon_{N})}\cdot\left(\frac{\pi}{\theta}\right)^{N}\cdot e^{-NF_{\min}}

Note that the maximal order of the errors is
e^{11N\varepsilon_{N}}
for all large enough
N
, which is beaten by
e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}}
as
N\to\infty
(since
\varepsilon_{N}\to 0
by our assumption). Combining our bounds we note that for
N
sufficiently large,

\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}))\leqslant e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}.

Next, we need to verify the existence of a
\delta_{N}\to 0
such that

\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})\subseteq\{x:\exists a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)\text{ s.t. }|x-a|<\delta_{N}\}.

Suppose there exists a sequence
N_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, an
x_{N_{k}}
and a fixed
\delta>0
such that
x_{{N_{k}}}\in\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N_{k}})
and
d(x_{N_{k}},\mathscr{M(\theta,\nu)})>\delta
. The sequence
x_{N_{k}}
is bounded, and every limit point
x_{*}
is at least distance
\delta
away from
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
. By definition,
x_{*}\notin\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
since
d(x_{*},\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu))>\delta
. On the other hand
F(x_{k_{N}})-F_{\min}=|F(x_{k_{N}})-F_{\min}|<\varepsilon_{k_{N}}
, and by continuity
G(x_{*})=F_{\min}
, which then implies that
x_{*}\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
, a contradiction.

\blacksquare

4.2
Free energy landscape

In this section we provide required details about the behavior of the functions
I,W
and
F
from
Section

1
. Many of these results are lifted from
[
13
,
28
]
and forms the core input in the phase transition behavior of the NLS field. We emphasize that results that are due to others are explicitly attributed to them, and the those that are not attributed are proved by us. Recall that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)=\frac{\pi}{\theta}-\inf_{a\in(0,1)}\{W(\theta(1-a))-\nu^{-1}I(\nu a)\}

I(a):=\inf\{\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{p}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}:\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}),\text{ }\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}=a\},

and

W(b):=\frac{1}{N}\log\int_{\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant Nb}\exp(-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2})\mathbf{d}\psi.

The free energy functional, which defines the free energy landscape is

F_{\theta,\nu}(a)=W(\theta(1-a))+\theta\nu^{-1}I(\nu a).

We start with the function
W(b)
because the required properties are relatively short. The fact that the defining limit exists and is continuous in
b
is proved in Section 4 of
[
13
]
. As a broad summary of Section 4,
W
is connected to the discrete Gaussian Free Field and is identified as free energy of the field conditioned to have given mass. As such, it can be expressed as a Legendre transform. For the purposes of this article, the required results are as follows.

Lemma 4.7

(Dey, Kirkpatrick, K. Lemma 4.7,
[
13
]
)
.

The function
W:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
satisfies the following properties:

1.

Convex, continuously differentiable and non increasing.

2.

Constant for all
b\in[C_{d},\infty)
, where
C_{d}={\sf G}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})
.

3.

Diverges as
b\to 0
, that is
\lim_{b\to 0}W(b)=\infty
.

For the second ingredient of the free energy landscape, we summarize several properties of the function
I
, which is the energy of a discrete soliton with given mass. The main result of
[
28
]
was to verify that there is a threshold for soliton formation for certain values of
p
, that is minimizers of
\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}-2p^{-1}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}
in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
exist only when the mass is larger than a threshold. Moreover, that this threshold is defined in terms of a functional inequality on the lattice. It is not hard to show that
I(a)\leqslant 0
for all
a>0
, all one needs to do is consider functions that are increasingly spread out. Define

(4.5)

\displaystyle R_{p}:=\inf\{a>0:I(a)<0\}.

Theorem 4.8

(Weinstein
[
28
]
)
.

The following hold.

(i)

An explicit minimizer of (
1.8
) in
\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z}^{d})
exists when
I(a)\in(-\infty,0)
.

(ii)

The threshold
R_{p}
defined above can be expressed as

(4.6)

\displaystyle\frac{2}{p}R_{p}^{(p-2)/2}=\inf_{\psi}\left\{\frac{\left\|\psi\right\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{p-2}\cdot\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{2}}{\left\|\psi\right\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}^{p}}\right\}.

(iii)

For
2<p<2+4/d
, we have
R_{p}=0
,
i.e.,

I(a)<0
for all
a>0
.

(iv)

For
p>2+4/d
,
R_{p}>0
,
i.e.,

I(a)<0
if
a>R_{p}
and
I(a)=0
if
a<R_{p}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Combined with the next Lemma, we can in fact conclude that
I(R_{p})=0
.

Equation (
4.6
) interprets
R_{p}
as reciprocal to the best possible constant for a discrete Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev type inequality to hold
[
28
]
. Basic analytic properties of
I
were verified in
[
13
]
. The only part required here is the following.

Lemma 4.10

(Dey, Kirkpatrick, K. Lemma 5.9
[
13
]
)
.

The function
I:[0,\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
is non increasing and differentiable. Further,
I^{\prime}(a)\leqslant a^{-1}\cdot I(a)

As discussed in Section
1.1
