, the free energy landscape describes the likelihood, on an exponential scale, of a certain fraction of the mass being concentrated. As such the minimizers of
F
represent the most likely values of this fraction. The set of minimizers
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)\subset[0,1)
characterizes the phases. As
\theta
and
\nu
are varied, we have two distinct phases.

Theorem 4.11

(Dey, Kirkptrick, K. Theorem 2.4
[
13
]
)
.

There exists a function
\nu_{c}:(0,\infty)\to(R_{p},\infty)
which is continuous and strictly decreasing, such that

1.

For
\nu<\nu_{c}
,
{\mathscr{M}}(\theta,\nu)
is a closed interval containing
0
, and is
\{0\}
when additionally
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
. This phase is referred to as
dispersive
or subcritical.

2.

For
\nu>\nu_{c}
,
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
is bounded away from
0
. This phase is referred to as
solitonic
or supercritical.

3.

As
\theta\to 0
,
\nu_{c}\to\infty
, and as
\theta\to\infty
,
\nu_{c}\to R_{p}
.

The characterization of the dispersive phase required for this article is slightly different and a little more refined as compared to
[
13
]
. The following two lemmas are to verify this characterization.

Lemma 4.12
.

If
\nu<\nu_{c}
and
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
, then
\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)=\{0\}
.

Proof.

Since
p>4
,
4.8
, we know that
I(\nu a)=0
on the interval
[0,\nu^{-1}R_{p}]
. Further, since
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
,
W(\theta(1-a)),(W(\theta(1-a)))^{\prime}>0
for all
a\in(0,1)
. Thus,
F
is strictly increasing in the interval
(0,\nu^{-1}R_{p})
and admits no minima on this interval. Suppose there exists an
a_{*}>\nu^{-1}R_{p}
such that
F_{\theta,\nu}(a_{*})=F_{\theta,\nu}(0)
. Observe that for any
\nu^{\prime}>\nu
,
F_{\theta,\nu^{\prime}}(0)=F_{\theta,\nu}(0)
, on the other hand
F_{\theta,\nu^{\prime}}(a_{*})<F_{\theta,\nu^{\prime}}(0)
since
\nu^{-1}I(\nu a)
is strictly decerasing in
\nu
when
\nu a>R_{p}
(see Theorem
4.8
). Thus, for any
\nu^{\prime}>\nu
,
(\theta,\nu^{\prime})
are supercritical, and thus
\nu=\nu_{c}
, which is a contradiction.

\blacksquare

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\theta>C_{d}
,
\nu<\nu_{c}(\theta)
. Then
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
is the closed interval
[0,1-C_{d}/\theta]
. Moreover,
1-C_{d}/\theta<R_{p}/\nu
.

Proof.

Since
\theta>C_{d}
, we know that
W(\theta(1-a))=W(\theta)
for all
a\in[0,1-C_{d}/\theta]
. Next, suppose
R_{p}/\nu<1-C_{d}/\theta
, then there exists
a\in(R_{p}/\nu,1-C_{d}/\theta)
such that
F(a)<W(\theta)
, which contradicts
\nu<\nu_{c}
. If
1-C_{d}/\theta=R_{p}/\nu
, then for any
\nu^{\prime}>\nu
,
(\theta,\nu^{\prime})
is a supercritical pair, implying that
\nu=\nu_{c}
. Thus, we know that
1-C_{d}/\theta<R_{p}/\nu
. For all
a\in(1-C_{d}/\theta,R_{p}/\nu)
,
F(a)=W(\theta(1-a))>W(\theta)
. Finally, suppose there exists an
a>R_{p}/\nu
such that
F(a)=W(\theta)
. Again, just as in Lemma
4.12
, for any
\nu^{\prime}>\nu
,
(\theta,\nu^{\prime})
is a supercritical pair, which contradicts
\nu<\nu_{c}
. This shows that
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)=[0,1-C_{d}/\theta]
.

\blacksquare

Proving that the local limit in the supercritical phase is massive, requires a brief bridging step, we verify this now.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\nu>\nu_{c}(\theta)
and
a\in\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)
. Then
\theta(1-a)<C_{d}
.

Proof.

The proof calls upon the differentiability of
W
and
I
. Since
0\notin\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
, we know that
\nu^{-1}I(\nu a)<0
for all
a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
, since
(W(\theta(1-a)))^{\prime}\geqslant 0
for all
a\in[0,1)
. Further, since
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)\subset(0,1)
, all
a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
are critical points of
F(a)
, which then implies that
(W(\theta(1-a)))^{\prime}+I^{\prime}(\nu a)=0
. By Lemma
4.10

\nu a\cdot I^{\prime}(\nu a)\leqslant I(\nu a)<0
, and thus
(W(\theta(1-a)))^{\prime}>0
. Finally, this tells us that
\theta(1-a)<C_{d}
.

\blacksquare

As the final result of this subsection, we prove Corollary
1.3
modulo the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Namely, we show that there is a range of
\nu
such that as
\theta
is varied with
\nu
fixed, we see a double phase transition. This requires only the results on the transition curve and the structure of
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
assembled here.

Proof.

We point out first that by Theorem
4.11
, since the transition curve is strictly decreasing,
\nu_{c}(C_{d})>R_{p}
. Choose
\nu\in(R_{p},\nu_{c}(C_{d}))
. Let
\theta_{c}(\nu)
denote the inverse function of
\nu_{c}
, which clearly exists and is continuous for the same reason. By our choice of
\nu
,
C_{d}<\theta_{c}(\nu)
. When
\theta<C_{d}
, the parameters are subcritical and item a. of Theorem
1.1
applies. When
\theta\in(C_{d},\theta_{c}(\nu))
, the parameters are still subcritical, but the limit is massless and item b. applies. Finally, when
\theta>\theta_{c}(\nu)
, the parameters are supercritical and item c. applies.

\blacksquare

5
Proof of
1.1

5.1
Local limit of the subcritical phase
\nu<\nu_{c}

Recall our strategy from
Section

1.3
. If
\nu<\nu_{c}
we want to first argue that the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
is
o(\sqrt{N})
, and then we want to bootstrap and push this bound down to
N^{\beta}
for some small
0<\beta<\frac{1}{2}
.
For
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
, we already know from
Propositions

4.6
and
4.12
that the
\ell^{\infty}
norm of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
is
o(\sqrt{N})
with high probability. The additional complication brought about in the regime
\theta>C_{d}
is the fact that the set of minimizers is now an interval containing
0
(
Lemma

4.13
) which prevents the immediate deduction of the effective
\ell^{\infty}
bound.

Key to the analysis here is the following inequality proved in
[
28
]
. Recall the definition of
R_{p}
from (
4.5
). Let
\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
, such that
\left\|\psi\right\|^{2}_{2}=a<R_{p}
, whence
I(a)=0
. Then

(5.1)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{2\nu^{(p-2)/2}}{p}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}\geqslant\left(1-\left(\frac{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}{\nu^{-1}R_{p}}\right)^{(p-2)/2}\right)\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}.

This inequality can be derived by using the fact that
R_{p}
can also be characterized as the optimal constant for the discrete version of the GNS inequality. We refer the interested reader to
[
28
, Section 4]
for more details.

In terms of
\mathcal{H}_{N}
, we may rescale to obtain the same inequality for all
\mathbb{Z}^{d}-
embeddable
\psi
whose mass is bounded above by
N(1-C_{d}/\theta)
, albeit with
\nu
replaced with
\nu_{N}
. Moreover, we know that for
a<R_{p}/\nu
,
I(\nu a)=0
, which further tells us that
\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\geqslant 2p^{-1}\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}
. Combining,

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi)\geqslant\frac{2\nu^{(p-2)/2}}{N^{(p-2)/2}p}\left(1-\left(\frac{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}{\nu^{-1}R_{p}}\right)^{(p-2)/2}\right)\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{N}
be as in
Proposition

4.6
.
Let
\theta>C_{d}
and
\nu<\nu_{c}(\theta)
. Then

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\Psi\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant N^{1/2}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}}\right)\leqslant e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}.

Proof.

We know from
Lemma

4.13
that
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)=[0,1-C_{d}/\theta]
where
1-C_{d}/\theta<R_{p}/\nu
. Also recall the notion of the separating set
U=U(\Psi_{N},\varepsilon_{N})
. By definition of the separating set, the
\ell^{\infty}
bound on
U^{c}
is at most
\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
, so we need to show is that
\left\|\Psi|_{U}\right\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}}\sqrt{N}
holds with probability approaching
1
as
N\to\infty
, appropriately fast. Also recall the set
\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})
introduced in Proposition
4.6
, which we recall here for convenience

\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}):=\{\psi:\frac{1}{N}\left\|\psi|_{U(\psi,\varepsilon_{N})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\notin\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N})\}.

where
\mathscr{M}(\varepsilon_{N}):=\{x\in[0,1),F(x)-F_{\min}<\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}
and
F
is the free energy functional defined in (
1.12
).
It was shown there in
Proposition

4.6
that there exists
\delta_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
such that

\Psi\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})^{c}\Rightarrow|N^{-1}\left\|\Psi|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}-a|<\delta_{N}\text{ for some }a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu).

for
N
sufficiently large. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left\|\psi|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}\in\left(N\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2p}},(1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta})N\right)\right\}\cap\{\left\|\psi|_{U}\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}}\}

Note that
\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})^{c}\cap\{\left\|\Psi\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
since obviously
\left\|\psi|_{U}\right\|^{2}_{\infty}\leqslant\left\|\psi|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\left\|\Psi_{N}\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N}))+\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}+\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{A}),

It suffices now to verify the bound on
\mathbb{P}\left(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{A}\right)
.
We bring in the same decomposition introduced in Proposition
4.6
, where
\mathcal{C}(A,\varepsilon_{N})
denotes the collection of
\psi
such that
A=U(\psi,\varepsilon_{N})
. We then have the following upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Psi\in\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\sum_{A:|A|\leqslant\varepsilon_{N}^{-d-2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}(A,\varepsilon_{N})\cap\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon_{N})^{c}\cap\{\left\|\psi\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}}\}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi)}\mathbf{d}\psi

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\sum_{A}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i+j\leqslant N^{1-\xi}}}{{iN^{\xi-1}\in(N\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2p}},a^{*}N)}}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,A}\cap\{\left\|\psi\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4p}}\}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A})}\mathbf{d}\psi|_{A}\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{j,A^{c}}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A^{c}})}\mathbf{d}{\psi|_{A^{c}}}

where
\mathcal{B}_{i,A}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{j,A^{c}}
are as in
Proposition

4.6
and
a^{*}=(1-C_{d}/\theta)
. Let
\left\|\psi|_{A}\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4p}
, and
\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\in(N\varepsilon_{N}^{1/2p},(1-C_{d}/\theta)N)
. Then we know by Lemma
4.2
that the smallest possible
\ell^{p}
norm of a function contained in this set is achieved by those having one site attain the
\ell^{\infty}
norm, and have rest of the mass equally distributed among the remainder of the lattice sites.
Note,
|U|\leqslant\varepsilon_{N}^{-d-2}
. Thus, if for
\boldsymbol{x}\in A
such that
|\psi_{\boldsymbol{x}}|=\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{1/p}
, the function values on the other lattice sites is given by

(N\varepsilon_{N}^{1/2p}-N\varepsilon_{N}^{6/4p})^{1/2}\cdot\varepsilon_{N}^{(d+2)/2}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}N\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2p}+\frac{d+2}{2}},

where the inequality holds for sufficiently large
N
. The lower bound for
\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}
is thus given by

N^{p/2}\cdot\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4}+\varepsilon^{-d-2}\cdot N^{p/2}\cdot\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{p}{2}\cdot\frac{d+2}{2}}\geqslant N^{p/2}\varepsilon^{3/4}.

Thus,

N^{-(p-2)/2}\cdot\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}\geqslant N\cdot\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4}.

By (
5.1
), this then tells us for all such
\psi
,

\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi)\geqslant\frac{2\nu^{(p-2)/2}}{N^{(p-2)/2}p}\left(1-\left(\frac{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}{\nu^{-1}R_{p}}\right)^{(p-2)/2}\right)\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}\geqslant C(\theta,\nu,p)N\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4}.

We may use this estimate to obtain the upper bound

\int_{\mathcal{B}_{i,A}\cap\{\left\|\psi\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{p}}\sqrt{N}\}}e^{-\theta\mathcal{H}_{N}(\psi|_{A})}\mathbf{d}\psi|_{A}\leqslant e^{-\theta CN\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4}}\text{Vol}(\mathcal{B}_{i,A})\leqslant e^{-\theta CN\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4}+o(N\varepsilon_{N})}.

Thus,

\mathbb{P}(\Psi\in\mathcal{A})\leqslant\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}}\cdot\left(\frac{\pi}{\theta}\right)^{N}\cdot e^{-C\theta N\varepsilon_{N}^{3/4}}.N^{1-\xi}e^{-NW(\theta)+o(\varepsilon_{N}N)}

The proof is completed by bringing in the partition function lower bound from
[
13
]

\mathcal{Z}_{N}\geqslant e^{O(N\varepsilon_{N})}\cdot\left(\frac{\pi}{\theta}\right)\cdot e^{-NW(\theta)},

which then implies that

\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}\in\mathcal{A})\leqslant e^{-O(N\varepsilon^{3/4}_{N})}.

\blacksquare

Now we upgrade the
o(\sqrt{N})
bound to
O(N^{\beta})
for
\beta
as per Corollary
4.3
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\nu<\nu_{c}(\theta)
for any
\theta>0
, and let
\beta
be as per Corollary
4.3
. Then

\mathbb{P}(\|{\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}}\|_{\infty}\geqslant N^{\beta})=O(\log N\cdot e^{-N^{\beta}}).

as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

We will refer to the proof technique here as the
dyadic argument
, it will make another prominent appearance in the proof of
5.4
which addresses the supercritcial case
\nu>\nu_{c}
. We know that there exists a
\delta_{N}
such that

\mathbb{P}(\|{\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}}\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{\delta_{N}N})\leqslant e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}

by
Lemmas

4.12
,
4.6
and
5.1
which address the
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
and
\theta>C_{d}
cases respectively. Now we define the following sets

\mathcal{P}_{j}:=\{\psi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}):2^{-j-1}\sqrt{\delta_{N}N}\leqslant\left\|\psi\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{-j}\sqrt{\delta_{N}N}\}.

Define
j_{\max}:=\lceil(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)\frac{\log{N}}{\log 2}\rceil
.
Note that

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}\|_{\infty}\geqslant N^{\beta})\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{j_{\max}}\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}^{\theta,\nu}\in\mathcal{P}_{j})+\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{N}^{\theta,\nu}\|_{\infty}\geqslant\sqrt{\delta_{N}N})\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{j_{\max}}\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}^{\theta,\nu}\in\mathcal{P}_{j})+e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}.

Now, for a given
\psi\in\mathcal{P}_{j}
, since
\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N
, by Lemma
4.1
, we know

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}\leqslant 2^{-pj}\cdot N^{\frac{p}{2}}\delta_{N}^{\frac{p}{2}}.

We may rewrite the expectations in (
5.3
) in terms of the spherical law by using (
1.5
).
Namely,

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Psi\geqslant N^{\beta})=\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{p}^{p})1_{\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}}\right)+e^{-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2}.

By Lemmas
3.3
and
3.1
, we have a sub-Gaussian tail bound for the spherical model for the cases
\theta<C_{d}
and
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
respectively. The case of Lemma
3.3
under consideration is the case with no boundary, that is
U=\emptyset
. For the application of
Lemma

3.1
, we only apply it with
b>N^{\beta}>\log N
below, where
b
is as in that lemma. Combining with (
5.5
) and the definition of
\nu_{N}
(
1.1
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{p}^{p})1_{\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp(\frac{2^{-pj+1}}{p}N\theta\nu^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\delta_{N}^{\frac{p}{2}}-2^{-2j}C\delta_{N}N)

\displaystyle=\exp(2^{-2j}N\delta_{N}(\frac{2^{-(p-2)j+1}}{p}\theta(\nu\delta_{N})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}-C)).

Regardless of what
\nu
and
C
are, since they are fixed constants the expression inside the exponential is negative for sufficiently large
N
when
j=1
, and thus for all
j\leqslant j_{\max}
. We may bound the sum by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{j}\exp(2^{-2j}N\delta_{N}(\frac{2^{-(p-2)j+1}}{p}\theta(\nu\delta_{N})^{\frac{p-2}{2}}-C))

\displaystyle\leqslant j_{\max}\exp(N2^{-2j_{\max}}(\theta\nu^{\frac{(p-2)}{2}}\delta_{N}^{p/2}-C\delta_{N}))

\displaystyle\leqslant j_{\max}\cdot e^{-CN^{2\beta}}.

The proof is completed by noting that
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)/\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)\leqslant 1
. Indeed, this can be easily seen by taking
\mathfrak{f}
to be identically equal to 1 in (
1.5
).

\blacksquare

The following corollary is almost immediate:

Corollary 5.3
.

For any
\theta>0
and
\nu<\nu_{c}(\theta)
,

\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}\to 1

as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

We know that

\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}=\mathbb{E}\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{p}^{p}).

Clearly
\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)/\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)\geqslant 1
, all we need to do is verify the upper bound.By Lemma
5.2

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{p}^{p})1_{\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta}}+\mathbb{E}\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{p}^{p})1_{\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{\infty}>N^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{p}^{p})1_{\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta}}\right)+\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}\mathbb{P}(\left\|\Psi_{N}\right\|_{\infty}\geqslant N^{\beta}).

By Lemma
4.1
and Corollary
4.3
we have more bounds on the exponential which tell us that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}

\displaystyle=(1+O(N^{(p-2)\beta+(1-\frac{p-2}{2})}))\mathbb{E}(1_{\|{\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta}})+O(e^{-CN^{2\beta}}).

By choice of
\beta
, we know that
(p-2)\beta+(1-\frac{p-2}{2})<0
. Finally, the subgaussian tail bound through Lemmas
3.3
and
3.1
allow us to conclude the proof, since

\mathbb{P}(\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta})=1-O(e^{-N^{2\beta}}).

\blacksquare

We are now ready to characterize the local limit of
\Psi_{N}^{\theta,\nu}
in the subcritical phase. In particular, we now show that the local limit is the same as that of the spherical model.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
, Cases a and b.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
be a fixed finite collection of lattice sites, and let
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{A}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded continuous function. Now, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi^{\theta,\nu}_{N})1_{\left\|\Psi_{N}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta}})+O(e^{-CN^{2\beta}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{N}(\theta,\nu)}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta)}\cdot\mathbb{E}(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p})\cdot\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})1_{\|{\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta}})+O(e^{-CN^{2\beta}})

By Corollary
4.3
, we know that

\mathbb{E}(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p})\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})1_{\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|\leqslant N^{\beta}})=(1+O(N^{(p-2)\beta+(1-\frac{p-2}{2})}))\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})1_{\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}\right\|\leqslant N^{\beta}}.

Next by Lemmas
3.3
and
3.1

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})=\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})1_{\|{\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta}}+O(e^{-CN^{2\beta}}).

Combining bounds and applying Corollary
5.3
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{N})=(1+O(N^{(p-2)\beta+(1-\frac{p-2}{2})}))\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})+O(e^{-N^{2\beta}}).

Finally by Theorem
1.2
,

\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N})=\begin{cases}(1+o(1))\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{m})\text{ when }\theta<C_{d},\\
(1+o(1))\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{f}(\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}+U\boldsymbol{1})\text{ when }\theta\geqslant C_{d},\end{cases}

where
U
is uniformly distributed on the disk of radius
\sqrt{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}
in
\mathbb{C}
, and
m
and
\theta
are related as per (
3.3
).

\blacksquare

5.2
Local limit of the supercritical phase
\nu>\nu_{c}

In this section, we prove the following theorem, which is a detailed version of
1.1
, item c.

Theorem 5.4
.

Fix
p>4
and
\nu>\nu_{c}
. The sequence of focusing NLS invariant fields
(\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N})_{N\geqslant 1}
admits subsequential limits in the local weak sense. Furthermore, each subsequential limit is a massive Gaussian free field with a random mass
\{\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{M(\Xi)}(\boldsymbol{x})\}_{\boldsymbol{x}\in A}
where
M=M(\Xi)
solves

(5.6)

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\kappa}}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}(\pi\kappa_{i})+M}=\theta(1-\Xi).

where
\Xi\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
and
M(\Xi)>0
almost surely. Furthermore if
\theta<C_{d}
, then
M(\Xi)<m
almost surely where
m
and
\theta
are related as in (
3.3
).

As mentioned in
Section

1.3
, the idea is to condition the field in the separating set and then prove a local limit theorem for the NLS field with boundary conditions. By
Proposition

4.6
, a positive proportion of the mass is concentrated on the separating set. The issue of course is that the separating set only gives us a boundary condition
o(\sqrt{N})
on its boundary, which needs to be pushed down to
N^{\beta}
for small
\beta>0
in order to invoke
4.3
. This will be done using a dyadic argument similar to the subcritical case, but the boundary conditions give rise to additional complications. We plan to consider a set
U_{1}\supset U
, still not too big, which will ensure the
\ell^{\infty}
drop off in mass via the dyadic argument. However, we need to be careful as extra mass might be lost if
U_{1}
is too big.

Given the above strategy, it is no surprise that we now need the following version of the random NLS field
\Psi^{\nu,\theta,f,U}_{N}
with boundary conditions. Let
U\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
and let
f:U\to\mathbb{C}
be a function. For any bounded, continuous function
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{\bar{U}^{c}}\to\mathbb{R}
(recall
\bar{U}^{c}=U^{c}\cup\partial U
).

(5.7)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi^{\nu,\theta,\gamma,f,U}_{N}))\\
=\frac{1}{\ Z_{N}(\nu,\theta,\gamma,f,U)}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{\bar{U}^{c}}}\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\exp\left[\theta\left(\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\gamma N}\boldsymbol{1}_{\psi|_{\partial U}\equiv f|_{\partial U}}\mathbf{d}\psi.

where the gradients are taken in the graph
G_{U^{c}}
, the subgraph induced by
\bar{U}^{c}
.

For some
A\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
and
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
, recall that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}(A,f,\varepsilon_{N})
denotes the following event:

•

U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\varepsilon_{N})=A

•

\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{A}\equiv f
.

Let
\alpha_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\|f|_{A}\|_{2}^{2}
. Recall that by the definition of separating set, we must have
\|f|_{\partial A}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}/10
.
The conditional law of
\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}
in
A^{c}
is given by
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta,(1-\alpha_{N}),f,A^{c}}
(NLS field with boundary condition
f
on
A
, defined in (
5.7
)) conditioned on the maximum absolute value of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta,(1-\alpha_{N}),f,A^{c}}
in
A^{c}
being at most
\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
. In other words, the conditional density is given by

(5.8)

\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\prime}}\exp\left[\theta\left(\nu_{N}\left\|\psi\right\|_{p}^{p}-\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi|_{A^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant(1-\alpha_{N})N}\boldsymbol{1}_{\|\psi|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\psi|_{A}\equiv f|_{A}},\qquad\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{\bar{A}^{c}}

for an appropriate partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\prime}(\nu,\theta,1-\alpha_{N},f,U,\varepsilon_{N})
(see
Lemma

4.5
). Let
\Psi_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}
denote this conditional random field, where we dropped the
\nu,f,A^{c}
from the superscript to lighten notation.

Similarly we introduce
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}
to denote the spherical law on
A^{c}
with boundary condition
f
and mass cutoff
1-\alpha_{N}
, and conditioned to take maximum absolute value
\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
. That is, the density of
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}
is given by

\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N},\varepsilon_{N})}\exp\left(-\theta\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi|_{A^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant(1-\alpha_{N})N}\boldsymbol{1}_{\|\psi|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\psi|_{A}\equiv f|_{A}},\qquad\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{\bar{A}^{c}}.

where
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\text{sph},N}=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N},\varepsilon_{N})
is the appropriate partition function.
Also, for any bounded continuous function
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{\bar{A}^{c}}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(5.9)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}})))=\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\text{sph},N}}{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{N}}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}})\exp\left[\theta\left(\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}\right\|_{p}^{p}\right)\right]\right)

Note that

(5.10)

\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{\text{sph},N}}{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{N}}\leqslant 1.

Now let us compare the law of
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}
to the same law without the
\ell^{\infty}
conditioning. The latter is already defined in (
3.1
) and let
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}
denote the random field with this law. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,1-\alpha_{N},A^{c})=\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N})
denote its partition function (simplifying the notation). Our next lemma states that these two partition functions are very close to each other if the mass of
f
is chosen correctly.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose
\nu>\nu_{c}
and
f,A,\alpha_{N},\varepsilon_{N}
be as above and assume
\alpha_{N}=a+o_{N}(1)
for some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)
. We have

\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{{\sf\text{sph}},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N},\varepsilon_{N})}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N})}=1+o(1).

Proof.

Note that

\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N},\varepsilon_{N})}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(\theta,1-\alpha_{N})}=\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N})=\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N})

where we used the scaling relation (
3.2
) for the second equality. To estimate this probability, we invoke the Gaussian tail bound in
Lemma

3.3
and an union bound. Recall that by the definition of the separating set, the boundary values of
f
on
\partial U
is at most
\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}/10
. Hence the massive harmonic extension is also at most
\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}/10
. Furthermore, note
\theta(1-\alpha_{N})\to\theta(1-a)
for some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
, and
Lemma

4.14
ensures that
\theta(1-a)<C_{d}
(this is where we crucially use the fact that the mass lost in
U
pushes the regime to the subcritical phase in the rest.) Thus
\theta(1-\alpha_{N})<C_{d}
for all large enough
N
. Thus
Lemma

3.3
is applicable to all vertices in
A
, and hence

\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N})=1+o(1).

This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

The following is the key proposition of this section, which significantly strengthens
proposition

4.6
. Given
U\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
, let

(5.11)

U_{1}=U_{1}(C):=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}:d(\boldsymbol{x},U)\leqslant C\log^{2}N\}.

Proposition 5.6
.

There exists a constant
,C>0
such that for all
\nu>\nu_{c}
and
\beta>0
the following holds. Let
\varepsilon_{N}=(\log N)^{-1},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}=(\log N)^{-10d}
and let
\delta_{N},\tilde{\delta}_{N}
be as in
Proposition

4.6
for
\varepsilon_{N}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}
respectively. Let
U(\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N},\varepsilon_{N})=U
.
Then the following holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cN^{2(\beta\wedge\frac{1}{2})})
:

a.

\frac{1}{N}\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{U_{1}}\|^{2}_{2}|\in(a-\tilde{\delta}_{N},a+\tilde{\delta}_{N})
for some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
,

b.

\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{U_{1}^{c}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta}
.

c.

\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{\partial U_{1}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta}/2
.

where
U_{1}=U_{1}(C)
is as in (
5.11
) if
\beta<1/2
and
U_{1}=U
is
\beta\geqslant 1/2
.

Proof.

If
\beta\geqslant 1/2
we are done by using
Proposition

4.6
. So
assume
\beta<1/2
.
Let

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\varepsilon_{N},\delta_{N}):=\{\frac{1}{N}\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{U}\|^{2}_{2}\in(a-\delta_{N},a+\delta_{N})\text{ for some }a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)\}.

Note
\varepsilon_{N}=(\log N)^{-1}>e^{-\sqrt{\log N}}
is a valid choice for
Proposition

4.6
to be applicable. Applying
Proposition

4.6
for our choice of
\delta_{N}
, we conclude

(5.12)

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})\geqslant 1-\exp(-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2).

Condition on the event
\mathcal{U}(A,f,\varepsilon_{N})
defined just before (
5.8
) and assume
\frac{1}{N}\|f\|_{2}^{2}=\alpha_{N}=a+o(1)
for some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)
(i.e.,
\mathcal{B}
occurs). Recall the notation
\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}
which denotes the conditional law of the NLS field on
A^{c}
conditioned on
\mathcal{U}(A,f,\varepsilon_{N})
.
Let
m_{N}
be related to
\theta(1-\alpha_{N})
as in (
3.3
). Since
\alpha_{N}
is close to some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)
, by
Lemma

4.14
,
\theta(1-\alpha_{N})
is bounded above and away from
C_{d}
, and hence we conclude
m_{N}\geqslant c_{0}>0
for all large enough
N
.

The idea now is to apply the dyadic argument as in the the subcritical case, but since the field can be actually large near
U
, we need to inductively do it at each dyadic scale to counter the tilt. We do so by looking at neighbourhoods of logarithmic radius around
U
and prove that for each such neighbourhood, the field goes down by a dyadic scale with high probability.

This leads to the following definitions. Let

A_{j}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}:d(\boldsymbol{x},A_{j-1})\leqslant C\log N\},\text{ for $j\geqslant 1$},

where
A_{0}=A
. The constant
C
will be specified at the appropriate juncture in the argument. We define the following events:

\mathcal{A}_{j}:=\{\psi:\left\|\psi|_{\partial A_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{-j-1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}\text{ and }\|{\psi|_{A_{j}^{c}}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{-j}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}}\}.

Note that
\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}^{\nu,\theta}\in\mathcal{A}_{0})=1
(see
Lemma

4.4
).
Let
j_{\max}
denote the smallest integer
j
such that
2^{-j}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}\leqslant N^{\beta}
. Note that
A_{j_{\max}}=U_{1}(\tilde{C})
where
Cj_{\max}/\log N=\tilde{C}
. Note
j_{\max}=O(\log N)
hence
\tilde{C}=O(1)
.

The goal for now is to prove that

\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{\max}})\geqslant 1-e^{-CN^{2\beta}}

To this end, define

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}=\bigcap_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{j}\text{ and }\mathcal{D}_{k}:=\mathcal{A}_{k}^{c}\cap\mathcal{C}_{k-1}.

and note that

\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{\max}})\geqslant\mathcal{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{\max}})=1-\sum_{j=1}^{j_{\max}}\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{\max}}\in\mathcal{D}_{j}).

We now concentrate on upper bounding
\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{D}_{j})
.
Pick function in
f_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
which is an extension of
f
on
A_{0}
. Condition on the event that
\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\equiv f_{j}
on
A_{j}
satisfying
\mathcal{C}_{j}
and let
\Psi^{j}_{N}
be the conditional law of
\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}
on
A_{j}^{c}
(dropping the subscripts and superscripts for clarity). By the same logic as in
Lemma

4.5
, this conditional law is given by an NLS field in
A_{j}^{c}
with boundary values on
\partial A_{j}
at most
2^{-j-1}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
in absolute value, the maximum absolute value on
A_{j}^{c}
being at most
2^{-j}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
and mass cutoff
1-\alpha_{N,j}
where
\alpha_{N,j}=\frac{1}{N}\|f_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. We now write

(5.14)

\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{D}_{j+1})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}^{c}_{j+1}))

where the expectation is over all possible
f_{j}
satisfying the constraints above. Let
\Psi_{\textsf{sph},N}^{j}
be the spherical law on
A_{j}^{c}
with boundary condition equalling
f_{j}
on
\partial A_{j}
,
\ell^{\infty}
-norm bounded by
2^{-j}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
on
A_{j}^{c}
and mass cutoff
1-\alpha_{N,j}
. Using analogues of
Equation

5.9
and (
5.10
) for our setup, we have

(5.15)

\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{j}_{N}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c})\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{1}_{\Psi_{\textsf{sph},N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c}}\cdot\exp(\frac{2}{p}\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{j}\right\|_{p}^{p})\right)

Applying Lemma
4.1
utilizing the
\ell^{\infty}
upper bound on
\Psi_{\textsf{sph},N}^{j}
, we find that

\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{1}_{\Psi_{\textsf{sph},N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c}}\cdot\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\left\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{j}\right\|_{p}^{p}))\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{2}{p}N\theta\nu^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{p-2}{2}}2^{-j(p-2)/2}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c}).

Recall that
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N,j}}
denotes be the spherical law on
A_{j}^{c}
with boundary condition
f_{j}
on
A_{j}
and mass cutoff
1-\alpha_{N,j}
(i.e.
\Psi_{\textsf{sph},N}^{j}
without any
\ell^{\infty}
-conditioning on
A_{j}^{c}
). Thus we can write

(5.16)

\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c})\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N,j}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c})}{\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N,j}}\|_{\infty}<2^{-j}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N})}.

Since
\alpha_{N,j}\geqslant\alpha_{N}
, we have
\theta(1-\alpha_{N,j})<C_{d}
. Hence
m_{N,j}\geqslant c_{0}
where
m_{N,j}
is the mass parameter related to
\alpha_{N,j}
through (
3.3
). Thus we can now apply
Lemma

3.3
to each term in the ratio (
5.16
). First note that because of the boundary condition, the harmonic extension (with mass given by
m_{N}
) is at most
2^{-j-1}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N}
. Therefore, an application of the spherical tail and union bound yields

\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N,j}}\|_{\infty}<2^{-j}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N})=1+o(1).

This is the reason why the boundary condition was chosen to be slightly lower than the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm condition. Furthermore,
we can apply
Lemma

3.3
for the tail bound of
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N,j}}
away from the boundary. It is at this juncture that we make our choice of
C
that define the
A_{j}
’s. Let
h
be the massive harmonic extension of
f_{j}
onto
A_{j}^{c}
with mass
m_{N}
.
Since
m_{N}\geqslant c_{0}>0
, we can choose
C
large enough such that for every
\boldsymbol{y}\in A_{j}^{c}
which is at distance at least
C\log N
,
|h(\boldsymbol{y})|<N^{-10}
. In particular, for
\boldsymbol{y}\in A_{j+1}^{c}
, we know that

\mathbb{P}(|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N,j}}(\boldsymbol{y})|\geqslant 2^{-j-1}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}N})\leqslant\exp(-CN\varepsilon_{N}2^{-2j-2}).

Overall, by the application of a union bound over all
\boldsymbol{y}\in A_{j+1}^{c}
,

\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c})\leqslant\exp(-CN\varepsilon_{N}2^{-2j-2}).

for potentially a different constant
C
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j+1}^{c})

\displaystyle\leqslant N\cdot\exp\left(\frac{2}{p}N\theta\nu^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{p-2}{2}}2^{-j(p-2)/2}-CN\varepsilon_{N}2^{-2j-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant N\exp\left(-N2^{-2j}\varepsilon_{N}(\frac{C}{2}-2^{-\frac{p-4}{2}}\theta\frac{\nu^{\frac{p-2}{2}}}{p}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{p-4}{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant N\exp(-C^{\prime}2^{-2j_{\max}}N\varepsilon_{N})

\displaystyle\leqslant N\exp(-C^{\prime}N^{2\beta})

where the second last inequality holds for
N
sufficiently large as
\varepsilon_{N}\to 0
(this is the reason why we needed the initial estimate of
Proposition

4.6
to bring the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm down to
o(\sqrt{N})
). Note that
j_{\max}
is
O(\log N)
, and thus plugging this estimate back into (
5.14
),

\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{\max}})\geqslant 1-\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\max}}\mathbb{P}(\Psi^{\theta,1-\alpha_{N}}_{N,\varepsilon_{N}}\in\mathcal{D}_{j})\geqslant 1-O(N\log N)e^{-CN^{\beta}}\geqslant 1-e^{-CN^{2\beta}},

where again the last inequality in the chain holds for
N
sufficiently large. Thus we have proved (
5.2
). Recall that since
\mathcal{A}_{j_{\max}}=U_{1}(\tilde{C})
, combining (
5.12
) with (
5.2
), we obtain

(5.17)

\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{U_{1}^{c}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta})\leqslant\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi^{\nu,\theta}_{N}|_{U_{1}^{c}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta},\mathcal{B})+\exp(-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2)\leqslant\\
\exp(-CN^{2\beta})+\exp(-N\sqrt{\varepsilon_{N}}/2)

We are almost done, except there is a gap between
U(\Psi_{N},\varepsilon_{N})
and
U_{1}
: we only know that the
\ell^{2}
-mass of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
on
U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\varepsilon_{N})
satisfies part
a.
while part
b.
is only satisfied outside
U_{1}
with high probability. We get around this simply by making a smaller choice of
\varepsilon_{N}
as follows.
First note that by our choice of
\varepsilon_{N}
,
the volume of
U_{1}\setminus U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\varepsilon_{N})
is at most

|U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\varepsilon_{N})|O(\log^{2}N)^{d}=O((\log N)^{4d+1}).

where the constant above depends on the choice of
\tilde{C}
and
d
.
Now choose
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}=(\log N)^{-10d}
and choose
\tilde{\delta}_{N}
as in
Proposition

4.6
. Observe that
U_{2}:=U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N})\supseteq U(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta},\varepsilon_{N})
. On the event

(5.18)

\mathcal{B}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N},\tilde{\delta}_{N})\cap\{\|\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{U_{1}^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\beta}\}

we see that the
\|\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{U_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}
satisfies item b. with

\delta_{N}=\tilde{\delta}_{N}+\frac{O((\log N)^{4d+1})}{(\log N)^{10d}}.

Indeed, on this event, the mass of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
inside
U_{2}
is in
((a-\tilde{\delta}_{N})N,(a+\tilde{\delta}_{N})N)
for some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)
and the mass in
U_{1}\setminus U_{2}
is at most

\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}N|U_{1}\setminus U_{2}|\leqslant\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}N|U_{1}\setminus U|\leqslant\frac{O(\log N)^{4d+1}}{(\log N)^{10d}}N.

This completes the proof since the probability of the event
\mathcal{B}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N},\tilde{\delta}_{N})
is at least
1-\exp(-c_{2}\sqrt{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}}N/2)
which is bigger than
1-\exp(-cN^{2\beta})
since
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N}
decays polylogarithmically and
\beta<1/2
.

\blacksquare

We now finish with the proof of
5.4
, which of course supersedes
1.1
part c.

Proof of
5.4
.

Choose
1/100>\beta>0
as in
4.3
.
Let
\varepsilon_{N},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{N},\delta,\tilde{\delta}_{N},c,C,U_{1}
be as in
Proposition

5.6
. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(C,\varepsilon_{N},\tilde{\delta}_{N},\beta)
be the event in
Proposition

5.6
, and hence

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})>1-\exp(-cN^{\beta}).

Let

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(f,A):=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\{U_{1}(C)=A,\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{A}\equiv f\},

and we only consider those
f,A
which makes the above event non-trivial, that is we restrict to the set

\mathcal{J}:=\{(f,A):\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(f,A)\neq\emptyset\}.

Fix such
(f,A)\in\mathcal{J}
and let
\alpha_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\|f\|_{2}^{2}
and note that since
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
occurs,
\alpha_{N}=a+o(1)
for some
a\in\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)
. As before, the conditional law of
\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}
given
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(f,A)
is
\Psi_{N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta,(1-\alpha_{N})}
. Let
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta,(1-\alpha_{N})}
be the spherical law on
A^{c}
with boundary condition
f
on
A
, mass cutoff
1-\alpha_{N}
and
\ell^{\infty}
-norm bounded by
N^{\beta}
.

Now let
\mathfrak{f}
be a function on a bounded set of vertices containing
\boldsymbol{0}
. Assume further that
d(A,\boldsymbol{o})>N^{0.1}
as required in
3.2
. Then first note,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta,(1-\alpha_{N})})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta,(1-\alpha_{N})}\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta,(1-\alpha_{N})}\|_{p}^{p})\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta\nu_{N}\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta,(1-\alpha_{N})}\|_{p}^{p})\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N,N^{\beta-1}}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}\right)(1+o(1))

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})})1_{\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}<N^{\beta})}(1+o(1))

(5.19)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}))+o(1)

Here, the second equality follows from
4.3
(which kills the tilt because of the choice of
\beta
) and the scaling relation for spherical law (
3.2
). For the third equality, note that the absolute boundary values of the field on
\partial A
is at most
N^{\beta}/2
(by item c. of
Proposition

5.6
), and
\theta(1-\alpha_{N})
is upper bounded away from
C_{d}
by
Lemma

4.14
. Letting
m_{N}
be the mass related to
\theta(1-\alpha_{N})
via (
3.3
), we conclude
m_{N}
is lower bounded away from 0 for all large enough
N
. Therefore the harmonic extension of the boundary condition with mass
m_{N}
has
o(N)

\ell^{2}
-norm by
Lemma

2.4
. Consequently, we can readily apply
apply
Lemma

3.3
and an union bound to conclude

\mathbb{P}(\|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta(1-\alpha_{N})}|_{A^{c}}\|_{\infty}>N^{\beta})<\exp(-cN^{2\beta}).

This, along with the fact that
\mathfrak{f}
is bounded justifies the fourth equality.

Now we average over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(f,A)
First of all let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
be the union over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(f,A)
such that
d(\boldsymbol{0},A)>N^{0.1}
. By translation invariance of the set
U_{\tau}
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})+o(1)
as
U_{1}
is of polylog order (deterministically). Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta})1_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}})+o(1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta})|(U_{\tau},\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{U_{1}}))1_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}})+o(1)

Note
(\Xi_{N})_{N\geqslant 1}:=(\frac{1}{N}\|\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{U_{1}}\|_{2}^{2})_{N\geqslant 1}
is a tight sequence of random variables, and has a subsequential limit. Take one such subsequence
(N_{\ell})_{\ell}
and call the limit along the sequence
\Xi
. Note that since
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})=1+o(1)
,
\Xi\in\mathscr{M}(\nu,\theta)
almost surely by the first item in
Proposition

5.6
.
Finally, on
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
, our choice of
A
is at least
N^{0.1}
away from
\boldsymbol{0}
as required by
3.2
. Hence, by (
5.19
) and
3.2
,

\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta})|(U_{1},\Psi_{N}^{\nu,\theta}|_{U_{1}}))1_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}})=\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\theta^{-1/2}\Phi_{M(\Xi_{N})}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}))+o(1)

where
M(\Xi_{N})
solves (
5.6
) with
\theta(1-\Xi_{N})
on its right hand side.
Since as
a_{n}\to a
,
\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}_{a_{n}}\to\Phi_{a}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
in law,
by dominated convergence theroem, we conclude that along the subsequence
(N_{\ell})
,

\Psi_{N_{\ell}}^{\nu,\theta}\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{(d)}\theta^{-1/2}\Phi_{\Xi}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

where the above convergence is in law in the local weak sense. Further properties of
\Xi
as asserted in the theorem are clear from
Lemma

4.14
and (
3.3
).

\blacksquare

6
Proof of
1.2
and results in
Section

3
.

In the following two subsections, we prove
1.2
, so it is useful to recall the definition of the spherical law
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}
whose density is given by (
1.4
). Also recall
C_{d}
is the Green’s function in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

6.1
The non massive case
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
.

In this section, we prove the second item of
1.2
. The proof proceeds through Fourier analysis.
Let
(\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}
and
(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}
be the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of
-\Delta
as in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
). Recall that
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{0}}=0
. Now we do the following change of variables (Fourier transform):

Y_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\langle\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta},\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle\text{ for }\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}.

Since this is a linear transformation and
(\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}}
are orthonormal, the Jacobian of this transformation is 1. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex valued function. Let
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}:=\langle\mathfrak{g},\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle
for each
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
be its Fourier transforms. Therefore

\langle\mathfrak{g},\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}\rangle=\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle

Now we wish to use the method of moments and prove that the moments of
\langle\mathfrak{g},\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{\theta}\rangle
converges to the moments of the desired Gaussian free field. However, we need to remember that our functions are complex valued, so we look at a more involved (but still elementary) expression involving the joint moments of the real and the imaginary part.

(\mathfrak{R}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle))^{q}(\mathfrak{I}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle))^{q^{\prime}}=\left(\frac{\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle+\overline{\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle}}{2}\right)^{q}\left(\frac{\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle-\overline{\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle}}{2i}\right)^{q^{\prime}}

for some
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
. The above product can be written as a sum over terms of the form

(6.1)

\alpha(Y,\iota):=\frac{1}{2^{q}(2i)^{q^{\prime}}}a_{i_{1}}a_{i_{2}}\dots a_{i_{q}}b_{j_{1}}\dots b_{j_{q^{\prime}}}

where each
a_{\ell}
is of the form
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}Y_{\ell}
or
\overline{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}Y_{\ell}}
and each
b_{\ell}
is of the form
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}Y_{\ell}
or
-\overline{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}Y_{\ell}}
. Each
\alpha
comes with a collection of indices
(i_{1},\dots,i_{q})
and
(j_{1},\dots,j_{q})
, all in
\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
, which we denote by
\iota
. Let
\Xi
be the set of all possible such indices, and we emphasize that repetitions are allowed.

Overall, we obtain

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}((\mathfrak{R}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle))^{q}(\mathfrak{I}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Y\rangle))^{q^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\alpha(y,\iota)\exp(-\theta\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}|y_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2})\boldsymbol{1}_{|y_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}+|y^{\prime}|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}d\boldsymbol{y}}{\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\exp(-\theta\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}|y_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2})\boldsymbol{1}_{|y_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}+|y^{\prime}|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}d\boldsymbol{y}}

where
y^{\prime}
is the vector
y
but excluding the coordinate
y_{0}
. Now we concentrate on one fixed
\alpha
in the sum. First observe that if there is a term of the form
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}y_{\ell}
which is not
paired with its conjugate,
\alpha
integrates to 0 because of the integral over the angular part of
y_{\ell}
. Therefore, we only need to consider the indices in
\Xi^{\prime}\subset\Xi
where each term in the product comes with its conjugate pair (therefore,
q+q^{\prime}
must necessarily be even for the integral to be non-zero). Thus we only need to consider product of terms of the form
|\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}y_{\ell}|^{2}
where there are
\mathfrak{s}:=(q+q^{\prime})/2
many terms in the product (with repititions allowed).

Now we write (
6.2
) in terms of expectations of random variables. Let
Z^{\prime}:=(Z_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}
be independent complex Gaussians, each with mean 0 and
Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}
having variance
\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}}
and let
Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}
be distributed uniformly on the unit disc, independent of everything else. Note that the
y_{\boldsymbol{0}}
term is missing in the exponent in (
6.2
) (since
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{0}}=0
), from which it is easy to see that (
6.2
) can be written as

(6.3)

\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}{\mathbb{E}(1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}

We take a moment to point out that with
Z^{\prime}
so defined,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}\phi_{\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}(\boldsymbol{x}).

where recall that
\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}
is the zero average GFF defined in
Section

2.2
. The following immediate consequence will be very useful later.

(6.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{0}\notin\iota)\in\Xi^{\prime}}\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)=\E(\mathfrak{R}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}\rangle))^{q}(\mathfrak{I}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}\rangle))^{q^{\prime}}

We now need a few estimates on the Fourier transforms. First of all it is easy to see from the form the eigenvectors (
2.3
) and the fact that
A
is a bounded set that

(6.6)

|\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ell}|\leqslant\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}

for a constant
C
depending only on
\mathfrak{g}
and the size of
A
. Let us consider a typical
\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\Xi^{\prime})
. It is of the form

|\hat{g}_{\boldsymbol{0}}\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2m_{\boldsymbol{0}}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}|\hat{g}_{\boldsymbol{k}}Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2m_{\boldsymbol{k}}}

where
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}2m_{\boldsymbol{k}}=q+q^{\prime}
. Note that we separated out the
Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}
as it is special: it has uniform distribution scaled by
\sqrt{N}
while the rest are complex Gaussians. Observe from (
6.6
) that

(6.7)

\mathbb{E}(|\hat{g}_{\boldsymbol{0}}\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2m_{\boldsymbol{0}}})\leqslant C^{2m_{\boldsymbol{0}}}\mathbb{E}(|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2m_{\boldsymbol{0}}})=C_{1}(m_{\boldsymbol{0}})

Also for any
\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
, a standard calculation if moments of complex Normals and (
6.6
) shows that

(6.8)

\mathbb{E}(|\hat{g}_{\boldsymbol{k}}Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2m_{\boldsymbol{k}}})\leqslant\frac{C}{(\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}})^{m_{\boldsymbol{k}}}N^{m_{\boldsymbol{k}}}}.

Since
(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}})^{-1}
diverges near the corners of the torus, we need to separate them out, and bank on the fact that the number of terms in the corners is small. This motivates the following definitions.
Now fix a small
\eta>0
. Define an
\eta
corner to be the set of all
\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
such that
k_{i}\in(\eta n)\cup((1-\eta)n)
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
. Let
\Xi_{\text{corn}}
be the set of elements in
\Xi^{\prime}
with at least one non-zero index in the
\eta
-corner.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a constant
C
depending only on
q,q^{\prime}
and the dimension
d
such that

\sum_{\iota\in\Xi_{\text{corn}}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota))\leqslant\begin{cases}C\eta\text{ when }d\geqslant 4\\
C\eta|\log\eta|\text{ when }d=3.\end{cases}

Proof.

From (
6.8
) and (
6.7
), we see that for every
\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}
,

(6.9)

\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)\leqslant C^{\mathfrak{s}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\iota,\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{k}N}

where recall
\mathfrak{s}=(q+q^{\prime})/2
. Thus we can write

(6.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{\iota\in\Xi_{\text{corn}}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\mathfrak{s}}\sum_{\iota\in\Xi_{\text{corn}}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\iota,\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{k}N}

\displaystyle=C^{\mathfrak{s}}\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\iota,\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{k}N}-C^{\mathfrak{s}}\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}\setminus\Xi_{\text{corn}}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\iota,\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{k}N}

\displaystyle=C^{\mathfrak{s}}(1+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}N})^{\mathfrak{s}}-C^{\mathfrak{s}}(1+\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}}{{\boldsymbol{k}\not\in\eta\text{-corner}}}}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}N})^{\mathfrak{s}}

By the Riemann sum formula and Lemma
2.8
, the last term above is

(6.11)

C^{\mathfrak{s}}\left[(1+\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}{\pi x_{i}}}\mathbf{d}\boldsymbol{x})^{\mathfrak{s}}-(1+\int_{[\eta,1-\eta]^{d}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}{\pi x_{i}}}\mathbf{d}\boldsymbol{x})^{\mathfrak{s}}+O(N^{\frac{2-d}{d}})\right]

The smallest
\eta
can ever be is
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}
, thus the
O(N^{\frac{2-d}{d}})
term will never corresponding to the leading order asymptotics. The order of this difference is the same as the difference in the integrals

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}{\pi x_{i}}}\mathbf{d}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{[\eta,1-\eta]^{d}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}{\pi x_{i}}}\mathbf{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

For
d\geqslant 4
, the asymptotics now simply follows by observing that

\int_{0}^{\eta}\int_{[0,1]^{d-1}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sin^{2}{\pi x_{i}}}\mathbf{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leqslant\int_{0}^{\eta}\int_{[0,1]^{d-1}}\frac{1}{\sum_{i=2}^{d}\sin^{2}{\pi x_{i}}}\mathbf{d}\boldsymbol{x}=O(\eta)

as the integrand over
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})
is integrable for
d\geqslant 4
. For
d=3
, we can calculate this by hand:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\eta}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sin^{2}(\pi x_{i})}dx_{2}dx_{3}dx_{1}

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\eta}\int_{[0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sin^{2}(\pi x_{i})}dx_{2}dx_{3}dx_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\int_{0}^{\eta}\int_{[0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2}}\frac{1}{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}dx_{2}dx_{3}dx_{1}.

The last inequality follows from the fact that
\sin(x)\geqslant 2x/\pi
for all
x\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
by convexity. Swapping the order of integration, and integrating over
x_{1}
first, we obtain

\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\int_{[0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}}\arctan(\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}})dx_{2}dx_{3}\leqslant\frac{\pi^{3}}{2}\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\arctan(\frac{\eta}{r})dr.

Integrating by parts, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\arctan(\frac{\eta}{r})dr

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\arctan(\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{2}})+\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\frac{r}{r^{2}+\eta^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\arctan(\frac{\eta}{2})+\frac{\eta}{2}(\log(2+\eta^{2})-\log(\eta^{2}))

\displaystyle=O(\eta|\log\eta|),

which completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Take
\beta\in\Xi^{\prime}\setminus\Xi_{\text{corn}}
. Let
Z^{\beta}
be the
vector
(Z_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\beta\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}
and
let
Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
denote the vector
(Z_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}\setminus(\beta\cup\{0\})}
.
In this notation, the constraint is of the form

\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}+\|Z^{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N.

Now we claim

Claim 6.2
.

For every sequence of
q+q^{\prime}
index choices
\beta=\beta(N)\in\Xi^{\prime}
,

\frac{1}{N}\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}\to C_{d}/\theta
and
\frac{1}{N}\|Z^{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}\to 0
in probability.

Proof.

The convergence in probability of
\frac{1}{N}\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}\to C_{d}/\theta
can be verified by showing that the variance of
N^{-1}\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{2}
converges to
0
as
N\to\infty
. We already know the limit by Lemma
2.8
. Note,

\displaystyle\var(N^{-1}\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}\var(|Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2})=\frac{1}{N}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\biggl(O(N^{1/3})1_{d=3}+O(\log N)1_{d=4}+O(1)1_{d\geqslant 5}\biggr).

The last equality follows from known asymptotics concerning traces of negative powers of the Laplacian, and can be verified by examining the corresponding Riemann sum. This is addressed in more detail in the proof of Lemma
6.4
, and we also refer the reader to Lemma 2.1 of
[
12
]
. Since
\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, it suffices to now prove that for any
\beta
,
N^{-1}{\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}}
converges to
0
in probability as
N\to\infty
. Note that
\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}
is the sum at most
q+q^{\prime}
many exponential random variables with mean bounded above by
N^{1/d}
. In particular,

\E N^{-1}\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N^{-1}\cdot O(N^{1/d})=O(N^{\frac{-(d-1)}{d}}).

Since
\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}
is positive, this completes the proof.

\blacksquare

The above convergence can be made almost sure by using less crude bounds. However, we do not require this.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
, item b.,
\theta>C_{d}
case.

First assume
C_{d}<\theta
. Then we have using
6.2
, for any
\beta\in\Xi^{\prime}\setminus\Xi_{\text{corn}}

(6.12)

\mathbb{P}(\frac{1}{N}\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\|Z^{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant 1|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\beta})\to 1_{|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}<1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}\text{ a.s.}

To see this, observe that by
6.2
, for any
\delta>0
, the above sequence of conditional probabilities converge to
1
if
|Z_{0}|^{2}<1-C_{d}/\theta-\delta
and to
0
if
|Z_{0}|^{2}>1-C_{d}/\theta+\delta
. Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary, the above convergence follows.

Using (
6.12
), we immediately conclude that the denominator in (
6.3
) is

\mathbb{P}(|Z_{0}|^{2}<1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta})+o(1)=(1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta})+o(1),

since
|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\sim
Unif
(0,1)
. Furthermore, employing the worst case of
Lemma

6.1
(twice), the numerator in (
6.3
) is

\displaystyle\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}\setminus\Xi_{\text{corn}}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{|Z_{0}|^{2}<1-C_{d}/\theta})

\displaystyle+O(\eta|\log\eta|)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{|Z_{0}|^{2}<1-C_{d}/\theta})+O(\eta|\log\eta|).

We have absorbed the
o(1)
term coming from
Equation

6.12
into the
O(\eta|\log\eta|)
term. Overall, the ratio (
6.3
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{|Z_{0}|^{2}<1-C_{d}/\theta})+O(\eta|\log\eta|)}{(1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta})+o(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}((\mathfrak{R}(\langle{\mathfrak{g}},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}+U\boldsymbol{1}\rangle))^{q}(\mathfrak{I}(\langle{\mathfrak{g}},

\displaystyle\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}+U\boldsymbol{1}\rangle))^{q^{\prime}})+O(\eta|\log\eta|),

where
U
is uniform over the disk of radius
\sqrt{1-\frac{C_{d}}{\theta}}
in the complex plane and independent of
\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}
. Note, we have used (
6.5
) here.
Since
\eta
is arbitrary the proof is complete by using Lemma
2.8
, the local convergence of
\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}
to
\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.

\blacksquare

We now turn to the
\theta=C_{d}
case, but this needs some preparation. First, we separate the indices, let
\beta
denote the collection of indices that are present in the monomial
\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)
. The ratio (
6.3
) takes the following form:

(6.13)

\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}+\|Z^{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N}}{\mathbb{P}(\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}

The problem now is that the denominator tends to zero, so we need to show that this divergence is canceled out by the numerator. A fundamental step here is to actually control the rate of convergence of the denominator to
0
, which involves two ingredients. The first is an estimate of the rate of convergence of the diagonal of the zero average Green’s function to its
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
counterpart, which was established in Lemma
2.8
. For the second ingredient, we need separate the cases of
d\geqslant 4
and
d=3
as they require different tools. For
d\geqslant 4
, we will essentially prove a Central Limit Theorem for
\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, by establishing the Lyapunov criterion.

Lemma 6.3
.

For
d\geqslant 4
,

\frac{\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\|Z^{\prime}\|^{2})}{\sqrt{\var(\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2})}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{(d)}N(0,1)

Proof.

We check the Lyapunov condition. Note that
(Z_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}
is equal in distribution to
\frac{X_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}}
where
(X_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}
are i.i.d. Exponential
(1)
. Thus to estimate the moments, we are left with computing the asymptotics of sums of the type
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{-q}
for integers
q
. One can check Lemma 2.1 of
[
12
]

(6.14)

\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{q}}\asymp\begin{cases}N^{\frac{2q}{d}}\text{ if }2q>d\\
N\log N\text{ if }2q=d\\
N\text{ if }2q<d\end{cases}

Using these estimates, it is easy to verify the Lyapunov criterion for
d\geqslant 4
:

(6.15)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbb{E}((Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\mathbb{E}(Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}))^{4})}{(\var(\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}}\to 0

This immediately gives us the required Central limit theorem.

\blacksquare

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
d\geqslant 4
and
\theta=C_{d}
. There exists
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)\geqslant\begin{cases}C\frac{\sqrt{\log N}}{N}\text{ when }d=4\\
\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}\text{ when }d\geqslant 5.\end{cases}

Proof.

By the Green’s function estimate of Lemma
2.8
,
we know that

|\E\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}-N|\leqslant CN^{\frac{2}{d}}.

Thus, the fluctuations of
\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, as characterized in Lemma
6.3
overwhelm the deterministic deviation of the expectation from
N
for all
d\geqslant 4
. In particular, this allows us to conclude that for
s>0
and
d=4
, by Lemma
6.3

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant\frac{s\sqrt{\log N}}{\sqrt{N}},\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-s\sqrt{N\log N})

\displaystyle=\frac{s\sqrt{\log N}}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{2}\leqslant N-s\sqrt{N\log N})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{s\sqrt{\log N}}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot(F_{W}(-s)+o(1)),

and for
d\geqslant 5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant\frac{s}{\sqrt{N}},\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-s\sqrt{N})

\displaystyle=\frac{s}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-s\sqrt{N})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{s}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot(F_{W}(-s)+o(1)).

Where
F_{W}
denotes the CDF of a standard real valued Gaussian random variable. Of course, both of these are bounded above by
\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)
.

\blacksquare

The CLT method fails in
d=3
, because the mass contributed by the low frequency modes is significant. We will use a more hands on approach here.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
d=3
and
\theta=C_{d}
. Then there exists
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)\geqslant CN^{-\frac{1}{3}}(\log\log N)^{\frac{1}{3}}(\log N)^{-1}.

Proof.

Recall that
n
denotes the side length of our discrete torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{n}
, and
N=n^{3}
. Also recall the notion of
\eta
-corner introduced in
Section

6.1
. We will denote by
\mathcal{S}_{N}
the
\eta-
corner, with the specific choice of
\eta=\frac{(c\log\log N)^{1/3}}{n}
for some
c>0
. Now, we may separate

\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}|Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\notin\mathcal{S}_{N}}|Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}.

We also introduce the notiation

\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}|Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\text{ and }\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}}|Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}.

Every
|Z_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}
is equal in distribution to
(\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}})^{-1}X_{\boldsymbol{k}}
where
\{X_{\boldsymbol{k}}\}
is an i.i.d. family of exponential random variables with mean
1
. Consider the event

\mathcal{V}_{N}:=\{X_{\boldsymbol{k}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}\}.

There are
2^{3}\cdot c\log\log N
vertices in
\mathcal{S}_{N}
, and thus the probability of this event is
(1-e^{-1/2})^{c2^{3}\log\log N}
, which decays polylogaritmically as
N\to\infty
We choose
c
such that this probability is
(\log N)^{-1}
. On
\mathcal{V}_{N}
,

\mathbb{E}\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}1_{\mathcal{V}_{N}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Using the explicit expression of eigenvalues
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, we find that

\displaystyle\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{\theta}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\frac{1}{4\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi k_{i}}{n})}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{N}{\theta}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{k}/n\right\|^{2}}.

The last inequality follows from the upper bound
\sin(x)\leqslant x
for all
x\geqslant 0
. We may directly compare this resulting sum to the integral of
\left\|x\right\|^{-2}
where
x\in[0,\frac{(c\log\log N)^{\frac{1}{3}}}{n}]^{3}
, excluding the cube with corner
0
and side length
n^{-1}
. Clearly, the above sum is the Riemann sum approximation to this integral. In particular, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\frac{1}{\left\|\boldsymbol{k}/n\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant C\int_{\frac{1}{n}\leqslant\left\|x\right\|\leqslant\frac{(c\log\log N)^{1/3}}{n}}\left\|x\right\|^{-2}dx\geqslant C(\frac{{c\log\log N}}{N})^{\frac{1}{3}},

where we use the fact that
\left\|x\right\|^{-2}
is integrable in a bounded neighborhood of
0
when
d=3
. Using the bounds
\sin(x)\geqslant\frac{2}{\pi}x
for
x\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and
\sin(x)\geqslant\frac{2}{\pi}(\pi-x)
for
x\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi]
(both bounds follow from concavity) yields a corresponding upper bound as well, and tells us that in fact

(6.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{N}}\frac{1}{\theta\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\asymp(\frac{\log\log N}{N})^{\frac{1}{3}}.

for some strictly positive
C>0
. Next, we consider the contribution of
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}
. Here, we need to bound two things. First, the gap between
\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
and
N
. The Green’s function estimate in
Lemma

2.8
tells us that

|\mathbb{E}\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}-N|\leqslant N^{2/3}.

Since
\E\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|=\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|
, we know that

\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|=N+O(N^{2/3})-\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2},

and by (
6.16
),

\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}-O(N^{2/3})=\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}(1+o(1)).

Next, the variance of
\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
. By the same bounds on the
\sin
function,

\var\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\notin\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\frac{\var(X_{\boldsymbol{k}})}{\theta^{2}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\leqslant\frac{N}{\theta^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\notin\mathcal{S}_{N}}\frac{1}{\left\|\boldsymbol{k}/n\right\|^{4}}.

Again, using the comparison to an integral, we find that

\var\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant CN\int_{n^{-1}(c\log\log N)^{1/3}}^{1}r^{-2}dr\leqslant CN(\frac{N}{{c\log\log N}})^{1/3}.

Note that
\sqrt{\var\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}
is of smaller order than
N^{2/3}
, and in particular is of smaller order than
\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
. By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\mathbb{P}(\bigl|\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr|\geqslant CN^{2/3})\leqslant\frac{C}{(\log\log N)^{1/3}}.

Now, consider the event

\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{N}:=\{|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{3N}\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{N}\cap\{\bigl|\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr|\leqslant CN^{\frac{2}{3}}\}.

On
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{N}
, by construction

\biggl(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\biggr)1_{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{N}}\leqslant N-\frac{1}{6}\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}(1+o(1))\leqslant N,

where the last inequality is clearly true for
N
large enough. For large enough
N
,
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{N}\subset\{\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N\}
. Using independence and the fact that
|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}
is uniform on
[0,1]
, we may calculate the probability of
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{N}
to obtain

\frac{1}{3N}\E\left\|Z^{\mathcal{S}_{N}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot(\log N)^{-1}\cdot\bigl(1-\frac{C}{(\log\log N)^{1/3}}\bigr)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}+\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N).

Applying (
6.16
) completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Now, we finally verify the convergence of the relevant moments.

Proof of
1.2
, item b. (
\theta=C_{d}
case).

We will need to consider two separate subcases, the first corresponding to indices
\iota
that exclude an
\eta-
corner, and the second the indices that are in the corner. The exact dependence of
\eta
on
N
will be specified at the appropriate juncture, and will be
d-
dependent. The goal is to show that the relevant moments converge to the corresponding GFF moments. For the corner, the goal is to show that the moments do not contribute in the limit. This includes
\boldsymbol{0}
, and there is no global shift like the
\theta>C_{d}
case. We begin with the first case, and focus on a single index
\iota
, so in this case
\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)=\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)
. Let
\beta
denote the collection of
\boldsymbol{k}
that appear in the monomial
\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)
. The term in question is

(6.17)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}.

Now, recall that

\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\beta}\frac{X_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}},

where
X_{\boldsymbol{k}}
are i.i.d. exponentials with mean
1
.
Define

\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}:=\{\left\|X^{\beta}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant K\log N\}.

We choose
K\log N
such that the corresponding probability of the complement vanishes polynomially, and the explicit choice of
K
will be made at the appropriate juncture. Note
\beta
contains at most
q+q^{\prime}
indices not from the
\eta-
corner.
Now, we may rewrite (
6.17
) as

(6.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}\cdot 1_{\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}^{c}}1_{{\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}{\mathbb{P}({\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}

The second term is the error and an upper bound suffices. Let
m
be a positive integer. Applying the Hölder inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}^{c}}1_{{\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N})}{\mathbb{P}({\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{c}_{N,\beta})^{1-\frac{1}{2m}}}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{\frac{1}{2m}}}({\mathbb{E}\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)^{2m}})^{\frac{1}{2m}}

Now we consider the first term. Since we are excluding points in the
\eta-
corner, the best bound we can get for
\|Z^{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}
on
\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}
is
O(\eta^{-2}\log N)
. We can rewrite the expectation by conditioning with respect to
Z^{\beta}
. However, before we do this, observe that for any any
z>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)-\mathbb{P}({\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N-Nz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(1-z\leqslant\frac{\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{N}+|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant 1)=z.

where the last equality follows from the fact that
Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
and
Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}
are independent, and
|Z_{0}|^{2}\sim
Unif
[0,1]
.
Recall that
\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)
depends only on the indices corresponding to
\beta
by definition. We express the numerator in terms of conditioning with respect to
\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|^{2}_{2}
. Let
p_{\beta}(w)
denote the density of
Z^{\beta}
. Applying the above estimate, we obtain

\displaystyle\E(\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}

\displaystyle\E(1_{\left\|Z^{\beta}\right\|+\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N}|Z^{\beta}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}\alpha(w)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|^{2}_{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}+|w|^{2}\leqslant N)\cdot p_{\beta}(w)dw

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)+O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})\bigr)\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}.

Thus, the numerator is

\bigl(\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|+|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)+O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})\bigr)\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}

For the denominator, the same conditioning argument as the numerator tells us that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\frac{\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{N}+|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant 1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\{\frac{\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{N}+|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant 1\}\cap\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta})+\mathbb{P}(\{\frac{\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{N}+|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant 1\}\cap\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}({\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)+O(\eta^{-2}\frac{\log N}{N})+\gamma_{N},

where

0\leqslant\gamma_{N}\leqslant e^{-K\log N}\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N).

Clearly,
\gamma_{N}
goes to zero strictly faster than
\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)
.
Thus, (
6.17
) may be recast as

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)+O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})\bigr)\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)}{\mathbb{P}({\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)+O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})+\gamma_{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\frac{O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)})}{(1+\frac{O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})+\gamma_{N}}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)})}\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}.

It is at this juncture that we introduce the bounds on
\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)
calculated in Lemma
6.4
. Recall,

\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)\geqslant\begin{cases}C\frac{(\log\log N)^{1/3}}{N^{1/3}\log N}\text{ when }d=3\\
C\frac{\sqrt{\log N}}{\sqrt{N}}\text{ when }d=4\\
\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}\text{ when }d\geqslant 5.\end{cases}

When
d\geqslant 5
, notice that with the smallest possible choice for
\eta
, namely
\eta=N^{-\frac{1}{d}}
, we have

\frac{O(\frac{N^{\frac{2}{d}}\log N}{N})}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}=O(\frac{\log N}{N^{\frac{d-4}{2d}}}),

which of course decays to
0
. When
d=4
, the worst choice of
\eta
is no longer adequate. Let
\eta=(\log N)^{2}\cdot N^{-\frac{1}{d}}
. Note that in this case, we have that

\frac{O(\frac{N^{\frac{2}{d}}\log N}{N\log^{4}N})}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}=O(\frac{1}{(\log N)^{\frac{5}{2}}}).

Finally, for
d=3
, we choose
\eta=(\log N)^{2}\cdot N^{-1/d}
, and

\frac{O(\frac{N^{\frac{2}{d}}\log N}{N(\log N)^{4}})}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}=O(\frac{(\log\log N)^{\frac{1}{3}}}{(\log N)^{2}}).

In all three cases,

\frac{(1+\frac{O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)})}{(1+\frac{O(\frac{\eta^{-2}\log N}{N})+\gamma_{N}}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)})}\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}=(1+o(1))\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}

An application of the Cauchy Schwarz inequality allows us to discard the indicator on
\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}
, since

\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}}=\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)-\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}^{c}},

and

\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)1_{\mathcal{A}_{N,\beta}^{c}}\leqslant\sqrt{\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)^{2}}\cdot e^{-K\log N}.

Combining all our bounds, we see that for indices excluding
0
,
