\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}{\mathbb{E}(1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}=(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)+e^{-\Omega(\log N)}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-\Omega(\log N)}(\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)^{2m})^{\frac{1}{2m}}}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{\frac{1}{2m}}}

We would like to point out to the reader now that since
\alpha
is a product of even powers of independent Gaussians, where each of the powers is bounded above by
q+q^{\prime}
, there exists a constant
C(q,m)
such that

(6.19)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}{\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)^{2m}})^{\frac{1}{2m}}\leqslant C(q,m)\mathbb{E}\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota).

We utilize this to further obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}{\mathbb{E}(1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)

\displaystyle+\left(e^{-\Omega(\log N)}+\frac{e^{-\Omega(\log N)}}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{\frac{1}{2m}}}\right)\E\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota).

Finally, we may choose
m
sufficiently large, so that

\frac{e^{-\Omega(\log N)}}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{\frac{1}{2m}}}=o(1).

Also remember that by construction,

0<\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\alpha(Z^{\prime},\iota)+\tilde{\alpha}(Z^{\prime},\iota)=O(1).

We now need to deal with the
\eta-
corner. We will repeat the trick with Hölder’s inequality. Note that for an integer
m\geqslant 0
to be chosen,

\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}{\mathbb{E}(1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}\leqslant(\E\alpha(\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime},\iota)^{2m})^{\frac{1}{2m}}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{-\frac{1}{2m}}.

Using (
6.19
), this is bounded above by

C(m,q)\E\alpha(\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime},\iota)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{-\frac{1}{2m}}

We note that (
6.19
) still holds despite the presence of
Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}
, since
\sqrt{N}\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\boldsymbol{o}}|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}
is bounded above and positive. We now sum over all
\iota
in the corner and apply the worst case of Lemma
6.1
to obtain the upper bound

\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi_{corn}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha((\sqrt{N}Z_{\boldsymbol{0}},Z^{\prime}),\iota)1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}{\mathbb{E}(1_{N|Z_{0}|^{2}+\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})}\leqslant O(\eta|\log\eta|)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)^{-\frac{1}{2m}}.

We can choose
m
large enough such that for all three cases of
\eta
and lower bounds of
\mathbb{P}(\left\|Z^{\prime}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)
, the above expression is
o(1)
. Finally, summing over all
\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}
and using (
6.5
),

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{R}\langle\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N},\mathfrak{g}\rangle)^{q}(\mathfrak{I}\langle\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N},\mathfrak{g}\rangle)^{q^{\prime}})+o(1).

This completes the proof.

\blacksquare

Recall that an important ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.1
in the subcritical case was the tail bound in the spherical law. For the case
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
this was Lemma
3.1
. We are now in a position to prove this.

Proof of
Lemma

3.1
.

Fix a lattice site
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{n}
, and note using (
6.5
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|\Psi_{{\sf sph},N}^{\theta}(\boldsymbol{x})|\geqslant b)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(1_{|\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x})+Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|>b}\cdot 1_{\left\|\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\left\|\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}\right\|_{2}^{2}+N|Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|^{2}\leqslant N)}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{O(\log N)}\cdot\mathbb{P}(|\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x})+Z_{\boldsymbol{0}}|>b)

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{O(\log N)}\cdot\mathbb{P}(|\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{0{\text{-}}\mathsf{avg}}^{N}(\boldsymbol{x})|>b-1)

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{O(\log N)}\cdot e^{-\frac{(b-1)^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-(b-1)^{2}/2+O(\log N)}\leqslant e^{-b^{2}/4},

where the first inequality follows from the bound in Lemma
6.4
, and the last inequality in the chain holds for all
N
large enough since
b
was chosen to be larger than
\log N
.

\blacksquare

6.2
The massive case
\theta<C_{d}

We know turn to the characterization of the local limit in the massive regime
(\theta<C_{d})
. We will also need to characterize the local limit in this regime with boundary conditions, which is Theorem
3.2
. Note that this is a substantial generalization from part a of
1.2
, which is for the case
U=\emptyset
. The proof of
3.2
, much like that of the massless case, relies on representing the relevant moments of the spherical law in terms of expectations involving an independent family of random variables with nice densities. Let
|U^{c}|=k
and let
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{k}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}}
consisting of eigenvectors of
-\Delta_{U^{c}}
(recall
-\Delta_{U^{c}}
is the restriction of the negative Laplacian to
U^{c}
, defined precisely at the beginning of
Section

2
). Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
be the respective eigenvalues, which are all non-negative. We will still refer to the operation of changing basis to
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots\phi_{k}
as the Fourier transform.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{T}_{n}^{d}
,
|U^{c}|=k
and fix any
m>0
. Write

\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}=\Psi_{1}+h

where
h
is the massive harmonic extension of
f
with mass
m
. Let
Y=(Y_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
where
Y_{i}=\langle\Psi_{1},\phi_{i}\rangle=(\hat{\Psi}_{1})_{i}
be the Fourier transform of
\Psi_{1}
. Let
\hat{h}_{i}=\langle h,\phi_{i}\rangle
. Then for any bounded continuous function
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(Y))=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\mathfrak{f}(Z)\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2})1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2})1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}

where
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
are independent and
Z_{i}
is distributed as a complex Gaussian with variance
(2\theta(\lambda_{i}+m))^{-1}
.

Proof.

First of all, we are going to write the density of
\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}
, first expressed
Equation

3.1
for
\gamma=1
, as below

\exp\left(\theta m\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}-\theta\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}-\theta m\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}1_{\psi|\partial U\equiv f|\partial U},\qquad\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{\bar{U}^{c}}

which will amount to tilting the law of the fourier transforms as we shall see.
Now we decompose any
\psi
with boundary values
f
on
\partial U
as

\psi=\psi_{1}+h

where
h
is the massive harmonic extension of
f
with mass
m
onto
U^{c}
. Note that
\psi_{1}
is identically 0 on
\partial U
. In the graph
G_{U^{c}}
(recall
G_{U^{c}}
is the subgraph induced by
U^{c}
)

\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{2}^{2}+m\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}=\langle(\psi_{1}+h,(-\Delta_{U^{c}}+m)(\psi_{1}+h)\rangle=\langle\psi_{1},(-\Delta_{U^{c}}+m)\psi_{1}\rangle+\langle h,(-\Delta_{U^{c}}+m)h\rangle

Indeed,

\langle\psi_{1},(-\Delta_{U^{c}}+m)h\rangle=\langle h,(-\Delta_{U^{c}}+m)\psi_{1}\rangle=0

since
\psi_{1}
is 0 on
U
and
h
is massive harmonic with mass
m
on
U^{c}
. Since
h
does not depend on
\psi_{1}
, we can write for any bounded continuous function
{\mathfrak{f}}:\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}({\mathfrak{f}}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}))=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{U}}{\mathfrak{f}}(\psi_{1}+h)\exp\left(\theta m\|\psi_{1}+h\|_{2}^{2}+\theta\langle\psi_{1},(\Delta_{U^{c}}-m)\psi_{1}\rangle\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi_{1}+h_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\mathbf{d}\psi_{1}.

where

\mathcal{Z}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}}}\exp\left(\theta m\|\psi_{1}+h\|_{2}^{2}+\theta\langle\psi_{1},(\Delta_{U^{c}}-m)\psi_{1}\rangle\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi_{1}+h\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{d}}\psi_{1}.

Define
y=(y_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
and
\hat{h}=(\hat{h}_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
to be the Fourier transforms of
\psi_{1}
and
h
, i.e., let

(6.20)

y_{i}=\langle\psi_{1},\phi_{i}\rangle=:{(\hat{\psi}_{1})_{i}};\qquad\hat{h}_{i}=\langle h,\phi_{i}\rangle=\langle h,\phi_{i}\rangle.

We can now write

\|\psi_{1}+h\|_{2}^{2}=\|y+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2};\qquad\langle\psi_{1},(-\Delta_{U^{c}}+m)\psi_{1}\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+M)|y_{i}|^{2}.

Since the transformation
\psi_{1}\mapsto y
is orthogonal, the Jacobian of this transformation has absolute value
1
. Now we define the random vector
Y
as the Fourier transform of
\Psi_{1}
where
\Psi_{1}:=(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}-h)
. For any
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can write
Y

(6.21)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(Y))=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}\mathfrak{f}(y)\exp\left(\theta m\|y+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}-\theta\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)|y_{i}|^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\|y+\hat{h}\|^{2}\leqslant N}\mathbf{d}y

where

\mathcal{Z}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}\exp\left(\theta m\|y+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}-\theta\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)|y_{i}|^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\|y+\hat{h}\|^{2}\leqslant N}\mathbf{d}y

Since

\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\theta(\lambda_{i}+m))}{\pi^{k}}\exp(-\theta\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)|y_{i}|^{2})

is the density of
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k})
as described in the lemma, we are done.

\blacksquare

At this juncture, just as in Section
6.1
, we would like to point out for the family of random variables
(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
defined in the proof of Lemma
6.6
,

(6.22)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\Phi_{m}^{U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{x}).

This allows us to relate the moments of
Z
and
\Phi^{U^{c}}_{m}
.

Proof of
3.2
.

Since
h|_{A}\to 0
because of exponential decay (first item of
Lemma

2.4
), it is enough to prove convergence of
\Psi_{1}
to the correct massive GFF limit using the method of moments. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex valued function supported on
A
. Note for any
\psi_{1}:\mathbb{C}^{U}\to\mathbb{R}

\langle\mathfrak{g},\psi_{1}\rangle=\langle y,\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle

where
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}=(\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
are the Fourier transforms of
\mathfrak{g}
and
y
is as in (
6.20
).
Using
Lemma

6.6
and the same decomposition as in (
6.3
), for any integer
q\geqslant 1,q^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, and any
m>0
,

(6.23)

\mathbb{E}((\mathfrak{R}(\langle\mathfrak{g},\Psi_{1}\rangle))^{q}\mathfrak{I}(\langle\mathfrak{g},\Psi_{1}\rangle))^{q^{\prime}})=\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Y,\iota))=\frac{\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z,\iota)\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}

where
\alpha(Z,\iota)
is as in (
6.1
) and
\Xi^{\prime}
is the set of indices where every term is paired with its conjugate in (
6.1
) (as defined after (
6.2
)).
It is now enough to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.7
.

Suppose
Z,\mathfrak{g},\hat{\mathfrak{g}},\hat{h}
be as above. Then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z,\iota)\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N})\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}=\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Z,\iota))(1+o(1)).

Indeed, given
Lemma

6.7
, we see that summing over all possible
\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}

\sum_{\iota\in\Xi^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Y,\iota))=\sum\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Z,\iota))(1+o(1))\\
=\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{R}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Z\rangle)^{q}\mathfrak{I}(\langle\hat{\mathfrak{g}},Z\rangle)^{q^{\prime}})(1+o(1))=\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{R}(\langle\mathfrak{g},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{m}\rangle)^{q}\mathfrak{I}(\langle\mathfrak{g},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{m}\rangle)^{q^{\prime}})(1+o(1)).

Since
\langle\mathfrak{g},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{m}\rangle
is complex Gaussian, its distribution is determined by the joint moments of its real and imaginary parts. Hence by method of moments,
\langle\mathfrak{g},\Psi_{1}\rangle\to\langle\mathfrak{g},\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Phi_{M}\rangle
in law. Consequently
\Psi_{1}
converges locally to
\Phi_{m}
as desired. The proof of the theorem is now complete, modulo the proof of
Lemma

6.7
which we prove below.

\blacksquare

Proof of
Lemma

6.7
.

Take
\beta\in\Xi^{\prime}
.
For a vector
y
, let
y^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
denote the portion of the vector
y
without the indices in
\beta
and
y^{\beta}
denote the portion of
y
with indices in
\beta
. We similarly define the corresponding quantities for
\hat{h},Z
:
\hat{h}^{\beta}
and
\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
and
Z^{\beta}
and
Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
. Then we break up (
6.23
) as follows. Write
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}
to be the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(\cdot|Z^{\beta})
. Then we can write the left hand side as

(6.24)

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z,\iota)\exp(\theta M\zeta(Z^{\beta}))\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2})1_{\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-\zeta(Z^{\beta})}\right)\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2})1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}

where

\zeta(Z^{\beta})=\|Z^{\beta}+h^{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}.

Let us concentrate on the conditional expectation of the numerator, the denominator can be estimated by similar logic. We will apply
a sharpened Lindeberg-Feller theorem (
6.9
) to the the independent random variables
(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2})_{i\in\beta}
, we postpone the exact statement of that lemma for now. The expectation and variance are easily computed to be

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2})=\frac{1}{\theta(\lambda_{i}+m)}+|\hat{h}_{i}|^{2}

\displaystyle\text{Var}(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2})=\frac{1}{\theta^{2}(\lambda_{i}+m)^{2}}+\frac{2|\hat{h}_{i}|^{2}}{\theta(\lambda_{i}+m)}

Now define

\displaystyle A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i\not\in\beta}\mathbb{E}(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2});\qquad A_{N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2})

\displaystyle B^{2}_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i\not\in\beta}\text{Var}(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2})\qquad B^{2}_{N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\text{Var}(|Z_{i}+\hat{h}_{i}|^{2}).

Let
p_{N}(u)
denote the density of
\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}
. Conditioned on
Z^{\beta}=y^{\beta}
(for some fixed vector
y^{\beta}
independent of
N
), the conditional expectation can be written as

(6.25)

\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-\zeta(y^{\beta}))}\right)=\int_{0}^{N-\zeta(y^{\beta})}\exp(\theta mu)p_{N}(u)du

At this stage, the choice of mass parameter
m_{N}
becomes important. We choose
m_{N}
such that

(6.26)

A_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\theta(\lambda_{i}+m_{N})}+\|\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}=N.

Note that

\|\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|h\|_{2}^{2}=o(N)

by second part of
Lemma

2.4
and our hypothesis on the boundary condition.
Combining this fact with (
2.5
) and
Lemma

2.5
yields
m(N)\to m(\theta)>0
as
N\to\infty
. In particular,
m_{N}
is bounded away from 0 and
\infty
uniformly in
N
.
Furthermore for this choice of
m_{N}
, and also from the fact that
\left\|h\right\|_{2}^{2}
is
o(N)
,

(6.27)

B^{2}_{N}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{i}+m_{N})^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{2|\hat{h}_{i}|^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+m_{N}}{=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{i}+m_{N})^{2}}(1+o(1))}.

Indeed (
6.27
) follows from
Lemma

2.6
(which tells us that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m_{N})^{-2}
is
\Omega(N)
) and

(4d+m_{N})^{-1}\cdot\left\|h|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{|\hat{h}_{i}|^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+m_{N}}\leqslant m_{N}^{-1}\left\|h|_{U}\right\|_{2}^{2}

Perform a change variables
w:=B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(u-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})
, so that the integral (
6.25
) becomes

\int_{-B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}^{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta}))}\exp(\theta m_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}))p_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}dw

Using
Equation

6.27
and
Lemma

2.6
, it is easy to conclude that

(6.28)

B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta}))=o(1);\qquad\frac{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}{B_{N}}=1+o(1)

We now claim

Claim 6.8
.

\int_{-B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}^{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta}))}\exp(\theta m_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}))p_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}dw\\
=\frac{1}{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\theta m_{N}}\exp(\theta m_{N}(A_{N}-\zeta(y^{\beta})))(1+o(1))

The main ingredient of the proof of
6.8
, which we postpone for clarity is a local version of Lindeberg CLT for triangular arrays. Assuming
6.8
for now note that exactly same analysis can be done for the denominator in
Equation

6.24
by assuming
\beta=\emptyset
. Thus
A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
in (
6.8
) can be replaced by
A_{N}
, there is no
\zeta(y^{\beta})
term, and
B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
can be replaced by
B_{N}
. This yields the asymptotics of the denominator as

(6.29)

\frac{1}{B_{N}\theta m_{N}}\exp(\theta m_{N}A_{N})\left(1+o(1)\right)

Plugging these estimates back in (
6.24
), we get

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left(\alpha(Z,\iota)\exp(\theta M\zeta(Z^{\beta}))\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2})1_{\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-\zeta(Z^{\beta})}\right)\right)}{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp(\theta m\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2})1_{\|Z+\hat{h}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N}\right)}\\
=\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Z,\iota)\exp(\theta m_{N}\zeta(Z^{\beta}))\exp(-\theta m_{N}\zeta(Z^{\beta}))(1+o(1))=\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Z,\iota))(1+o(1))

In the last line, we used
\mathbb{E}(\alpha(Z,\iota))
is bounded since
\mathfrak{g}
is bounded and
Z
is complex Gaussian. This completes the proof of
Lemma

6.7
modulo
6.8
.

\blacksquare

To prove
6.8
, we need the following refined version of the Lindeberg-Feller Theorem which in addition yields uniform convergence of densities. We refer the reader to Appendix A4 of
[
2
]
for a proof, although the result is attributed to Kolmogorov and Gnedenko
[
15
]
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
\{Z_{i,N}\}_{i=1}^{N}
be a triangular array of centered, independent
\mathbb{R}^{n}
valued random variables with density given by
\{q_{i,N}(x)\}
. Assume that the following conditions hold.

1.

For all
i
,
q_{i,N}
are in
L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
where
r\in(1,2]
is independent of
i,N
. Moreover,
\sup_{i,N}\left\|q_{i,N}\right\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant M
for some
M>0
.

2.

For all
i\leqslant N
,
\sigma_{i,N}^{2}=\var Z_{i,N}
are uniformly bounded away from
0
for all
i,N
.

3.

Let
B^{2}_{N}:=\sum_{i\leqslant N}\sigma_{i,N}^{2}
. Lindeberg’s condition holds, namely for all
\varepsilon>0

\frac{\sum_{i\leqslant N}\mathbb{E}(Z_{i,N})^{2}1_{|Z_{i,N}|\geqslant\varepsilon B_{N}}}{B_{N}^{2}}\to 0

Then, if
p_{N}
denotes the distribution of the sum
\sum_{i\leqslant N}Z_{i,N}
, we have that

B_{N}\cdot p_{N}(B_{N}x+A_{N})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)+v_{N},

Where
v_{N}
decays to
0
as
N\to\infty
uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of
6.8
.

We will apply
6.9
to
\|Z^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}+\hat{h}^{\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\|_{2}^{2}
whose density is
p_{N}
. Since
Z_{i}
s are exponential with variance
(\lambda_{i}+m)^{-1}\leqslant m^{-1}
and
m>0
, it is straightforward to apply check that all the conditions in
6.9
is verified.
Pick a large number
T
, and we break up the integral into an integral from
(-T,B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta}))
and the rest. Let
p
denote the density of standard (real) Normal random variable. Using
6.9
, the former can be written as

\exp(\theta m_{N}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})\int_{-T}^{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta}))}\exp(\theta m_{N}B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w)p(w)(1+o(1))dw

Observe the replacement of
B_{N}p_{N}(B_{N}x+A_{N})
by
p(w)(1+o(1))
, does not follow from Lemma
6.9
for all
w
. However, note that for all
|x|\leqslant T
,
p(x)\geqslant p(|T|)>0
. Thus, on this interval we know that
B_{N}p_{N}(B_{N}x+A_{N})=p(x)(1+\frac{v_{N}}{p(|T|)})
, which justifies the above approximation.

Next, by a change of variable,

\int_{-T}^{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta}))}\exp(\theta m_{N}B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w)p(w)dw=\int_{-TB_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}^{A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta})}\exp(\theta m_{N}w_{1})p(\frac{w_{1}}{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}})\frac{1}{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}dw_{1}

By dominated convergence theorem,

\int_{-TB_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}^{A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta})}\exp(\theta m_{N}w_{1})p(\frac{w_{1}}{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}})dw_{1}=\frac{1}{\theta m_{N}}\exp(\theta m_{N}(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta})))(1+o(1)).

Finally, for the integral from
-B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}
to
-T
we need the fact

p_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\leqslant C

for some universal constant
C
independent of everything else, which holds due to the uniform convergence of
B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}p_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}x+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})
. Then, since
B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\to\infty
(as
A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}=\Omega(N)
and
B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}=\Omega(\sqrt{N})
)

\int_{-B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}^{-1}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}^{-T}\exp(\theta m_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}))p_{N}(B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}w+A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}dw\leqslant C\frac{\exp(\theta m_{N}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\theta m_{N}TB_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})}{\theta mB_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}.

So we get an upper bound

C\exp(\theta mA_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})\frac{1}{\theta m_{N}B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}}\exp(-\theta m_{N}TB_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})

Combining with the first estimate of (
6.28
), we see that the integral (
6.25
) is

(6.30)

\frac{1}{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\theta m_{N}}\exp(\theta m_{N}A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}})\Big[\left(\exp(\theta m(A_{N}-A_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}-\zeta(y^{\beta})))\right)(1+o(1))\Big]\\
=\frac{1}{B_{N,\text{\sout{$\beta$}}}\theta m_{N}}\exp(\theta m_{N}(A_{N}-\zeta(y^{\beta})))(1+o(1))

as desired.

\blacksquare

Much as in the massless case, we conclude this section with the proof of the tail bound, which for
\theta<C_{d}
is
Lemma

3.3
. While the lemma we needed was extremely straightforward for
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
, the situation is more complicated here. The presence of boundary conditions and that we require the tail bound away from the harmonic extension near the boundary requires more care.

Proof of
Lemma

3.3
.

For this proof, we recall the notation
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c})
for the partition function of the spherical law with general boundary condition
f
and general mass cutoff
\gamma N
, defined in
Equation

3.1
, which we recall here for convenience:

(6.31)

\mathbb{E}(\mathfrak{f}(\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c}})):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,\gamma,U^{c})}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}}}\mathfrak{f}(\psi)\exp\left(-\theta\left\|\nabla\psi|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi|_{U^{c}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\gamma N}1_{\psi|_{\partial U}\equiv f_{|\partial U}}\mathbf{d}\psi.

Let
f_{w}
be a function defined on
U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}
which equals
f
on
U
and equals
w\in\mathbb{C}
on
\boldsymbol{y}\in U^{c}
. Then we can write

\mathbb{P}(|\Psi_{\mathsf{sph},N}^{f,\theta,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{y})-h(\boldsymbol{y})|>b)=\int_{|z|>b}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f_{h(\boldsymbol{y})+z},\theta,1-\frac{|h(\boldsymbol{y})+z|^{2}}{N},U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\})}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,U^{c})}dz

where the numerator of the integrand is taken to be 0 in case
|h(\boldsymbol{y})+z|^{2}/N\geqslant 1
. We now compute the asymptotics of the integrand, and let us consider the numerator first. We will do an analysis similar to the one done in
3.2
, but we need to be careful with the cancellations of the harmonic function terms as they are different in the numerator and the denominator. Choose
m_{N}=m
as in (
6.26
) (the dependence on
N
is suppressed here for clarity). All the Laplacians are computed in the graph induced by
U^{c}\cup\partial U
. Breaking up any
\psi:U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}\to\mathbb{C}
with boundary condition given by
f_{w}
into
\psi_{1}+h_{w}
where
h_{w}
is the harmonic extension of
f_{w}
with mass
m
onto
U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}
, we can rewrite the numerator, with
w=z+h(\boldsymbol{y})
as

(6.32)

C(h_{w})\int_{\mathbb{C}^{U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}}\exp\left(\theta m\|\psi_{1}+h_{w}\|_{2}^{2}+\theta\langle\psi_{1},(\Delta_{U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}-m)\psi_{1}\rangle\right)\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\|\psi_{1}+h_{w}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-|w|^{2}}\mathbf{d}\psi_{1}.

where

(6.33)

C(h_{w}):=\exp(\theta\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})})

In the above, the expression for
h_{w}
simplified because of the cancellation

(6.34)

\theta\langle h_{w},(\Delta-m)h_{w}\rangle+\theta m\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}|h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}=\theta\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})}.

Equation

6.34
is easily justified since
h_{w}
is massive harmonic in
U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}
with mass
m
and the fact that the integral in (
6.33
) is over
U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}
which justifies the second term in the left hand side in (
6.34
). Let
|U^{c}|=k
and let
(\lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{k},(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k-1}
be the eigenvalues of the negative Dirichlet Laplacian on
U^{c}
and
U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}
respectively.
In the same vein as
Lemma

6.6
, we can write
(
6.32
) as

C_{1}(h_{w})\mathbb{E}(\exp(\theta m\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-|w|^{2}})).

where
(Z^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Z^{\prime}_{k-1})
are independent and
Z^{\prime}_{i}
is a complex Gaussian with variance
(2\theta(\lambda^{\prime}_{i}+m))^{-1}
and

C_{1}(h_{w})=\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{2\pi}{\theta(\lambda_{i}^{\prime}+m)}\exp(\theta\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})})

Similarly the denominator can also be written in terms of complex Gaussians, and in fact we have already analyzed its asymptotics in (
6.29
), which are given by

(6.35)

\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,U)=C_{1}(h)\frac{1}{B_{N}\theta m}\exp(\theta mN)\left(1+o(1)\right)

where
B_{N}
is as in the proof of
3.2
and

C_{1}(h)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{2\pi}{\theta(\lambda_{i}+m)}\exp(\theta\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}h(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h(\boldsymbol{x})}).

Let us now compute the asymptotics of the numerator and compare. Let

A_{N}^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}(\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2});\qquad(B_{N}^{\prime})^{2}=\text{Var}(\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2})

Then, letting
p_{N}^{\prime}
to be the density of
\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2}
,

(6.36)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\exp(\theta m\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2}1_{\|Z^{\prime}+\hat{h}_{w}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant N-|w|^{2}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{N-|w|^{2}}e^{\theta mu}p^{\prime}_{N}(u)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-A_{N}^{\prime}/B_{N}^{\prime}}^{(B_{N}^{\prime})^{-1}(N-|w|^{2}-A_{N}^{\prime})}\exp(\theta m(B_{N}^{\prime}w+A_{N}^{\prime}))p^{\prime}_{N}(B_{N}^{\prime}w+A_{N}^{\prime})B_{N}^{\prime}dw

(6.37)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\frac{\exp(\theta m(N-|w|^{2}))}{B_{N}^{\prime}\theta m}

where we used again the uniform bound on the density
p_{N}
.
Now we need to take care of the harmonic function terms. We can break up
h_{w}=h+g
where
g
is the massive harmonic extension of the function taking value
w-h(\boldsymbol{y})=z
in
\boldsymbol{y}
and 0 on
\partial U
. Then it is straightforward computation to see

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}h(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h(\boldsymbol{x})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{x})}+h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y})}.

We deal with the two terms above separately. Let
(X^{\boldsymbol{u}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
be a simple random walk on
G_{U}
started at
\boldsymbol{u}\in U^{c}
which is killed at a vertex
\boldsymbol{u}
with probability
m/(\deg(\boldsymbol{u})+m)
and
\tau
is the first time when
(X^{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
either gets killed or hits
\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}
. Using the killed random walk description of the massive harmonic function,
Now we can write

(6.38)

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{x})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{x}}\overline{g(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})}=\bar{z}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{x}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{y})

By the reversibility of the killed walk, we see that

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{x}}\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau}^{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{x}).

Therefore

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{x})}=\bar{z}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{x})

Since
h_{w}
is massive harmonic in
U^{c}
,

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{x})=h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})-w\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=w).

Therefore, we simplify to

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\partial U}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta g(x)}

\displaystyle=\bar{z}\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}(h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})-w\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{y}))

\displaystyle=\bar{z}\left[\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y})+\deg(\boldsymbol{y})h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y})-(\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})+\deg(\boldsymbol{y})g(\boldsymbol{y}))-h(\boldsymbol{y})\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=w)\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{z}\left[mh(\boldsymbol{y})+\deg(\boldsymbol{y})h(\boldsymbol{y})-h(\boldsymbol{y})\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=w)\right].

\displaystyle=m\bar{z}h(\boldsymbol{y})-h(\boldsymbol{y})\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}

Now for the other term,

\displaystyle h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{y})}

\displaystyle=w(\overline{\Delta h(\boldsymbol{y})}+\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})})

\displaystyle=w(m\overline{h(\boldsymbol{y})}+\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})})

\displaystyle=mz\overline{h(\boldsymbol{y})}+m|h(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}+w\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}

Combining, we get

(6.39)

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U\cup\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial U}h(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\Delta h(\boldsymbol{x})}

(6.40)

\displaystyle=m\bar{z}h(\boldsymbol{y})-h(\boldsymbol{y})\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}+mz\overline{h(\boldsymbol{y})}+m|h(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}+w\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}

(6.41)

\displaystyle=2m\mathfrak{R}(\bar{z}h(\boldsymbol{y}))+m|h(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}+z\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}

To combine this with (
6.37
), we need to subtract
m|w|^{2}
from the last expression above. This simplifies to

\displaystyle 2m\mathfrak{R}(\bar{z}h(\boldsymbol{y}))+m|h(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}+z\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}-m|w|^{2}=

\displaystyle z\overline{\Delta g(\boldsymbol{y})}-m|z|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|z|^{2}(\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\sim\boldsymbol{y}}\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}}_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{y})-\deg(\boldsymbol{y})-m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-|z|^{2}(\deg(\boldsymbol{y})+m)(1-\mathbb{P}(X^{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\tau_{+}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-|z|^{2}(\deg(\boldsymbol{y})+m)({\sf G}^{m,U}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y}))^{-1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-c_{1}|z|^{2}

where
{\sf G}^{m,U^{c}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y})
is the Green’s function for the killed random walk.
Overall, we get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}\biggl(f_{h(\boldsymbol{y})+z},\theta,1-\frac{|h(\boldsymbol{y})+z|^{2}}{N},U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}\biggr)}/{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,U^{c})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\theta}{2\pi}\frac{B_{N}}{B_{N}^{\prime}}\frac{C\exp(\theta mN}{\exp(\theta mN)\left(1+o(1)\right)}\exp(-c_{1}|z|^{2})\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{\prime}_{i}+m)}

We finally claim

\frac{B_{N}^{\prime}}{B_{N}}=O(1)\text{ and }\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{\prime}_{i}+m)}=O(1).

For this, observe that
\Delta_{U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\}}
is a principal sub matrix of
\Delta_{U^{c}}
of dimension
|U^{c}|-1
, and thus we have the following interlacing relation for the eigenvalues
\forall i\in\{1,2,\ldots|U|-1\}
:

\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\leqslant\lambda_{i+1}.

For any bounded monotonic function
\mathfrak{r}:[0,4d]\to\mathbb{R}
, note that

\displaystyle|\sum_{i=1}\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{i})-\sum_{i=1}\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=|\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{|U|})+\sum_{i}\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{i})-\mathfrak{r}(\lambda^{\prime}_{i})|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{|U|})|+|\sum_{i}(\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})-\mathfrak{r}(\lambda_{i}))|\leqslant 2\|{\mathfrak{r}}\|_{\infty}.

We apply this to the functions
\log(x+m)
and
(x+m)^{-2}
. Thus, we have that

(6.42)

\displaystyle|\sum_{i}\log(\lambda_{i}+m)-\sum_{i}\log(\lambda_{i}+m)|\leqslant 2\log(4d+m),

and

(6.43)

\displaystyle|\sum_{k}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{i}+m)^{2}}-\sum_{k}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{i}^{\prime}+m)^{2}}|\leqslant\frac{2}{m^{2}}.

What this yields is that

|\log\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{\prime}_{i}+m)}|\leqslant 2\log(m+4d),

and in particular,

(m+4d)^{-2}\leqslant\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}+m)}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{\prime}_{i}+m)}\leqslant(m+4d)^{2}.

As for the ratio
B_{N}/B_{N}^{\prime}
, note that

\frac{B_{N}}{B_{N}^{\prime}}=\frac{\sum\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k}+m)^{2}}+\sum\frac{|\hat{h}|^{2}}{(\lambda_{k}+m)}}{\sum\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k}^{\prime}+m)^{2}}+\sum\frac{|\hat{h}^{\prime}|^{2}}{(\lambda_{k}^{\prime}+m)}}\leqslant\frac{\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+m)^{-2}+m^{-1}\left\|h\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\sum_{k}(\lambda_{k}+m)^{-2}-2m^{-2}+(4d+m)^{-1}\left\|h^{w}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\leqslant 2.

The last inequality holds for
N
sufficiently large. Combined, these yield the Gaussian upper bound (independent of
h
)

\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f_{h(\boldsymbol{y})+z},\theta,1-\frac{|h(\boldsymbol{y})+z|^{2}}{N},U^{c}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{y}\})}{\mathcal{Z}_{\text{sph},N}(f,\theta,U^{c})}\leqslant Ce^{-c_{1}|z|^{2}}

for some
C,c>0
independent of everything else. Integrating from
b
to
\infty
, we get the required tail bound.

\blacksquare

7
Future work and open questions

Over the course of working on this article, we came across several interesting questions which are yet to be answered.

1.

Non mixture in supercritical:
If
\nu>\nu_{c}
, we anticipate that when
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)=\{a_{*}\}
for some
a_{*}>0
, and that in particular,
\Xi=a_{*}
almost surely. The local limit should not be a non trivial mixture of Gaussians in the super critical regime. This requires more information on the function
I
, in particular properties of higher derivatives.

2.

Mixture in Critical:
For
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
, there is discontinuity in the phase transition in the following sense.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\theta\leqslant C_{d}
, and
\nu=\nu_{c}
. Then
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)=\{0\}\sqcup\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
where
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
is non empty and bounded away from zero.

Proof.

A key point to the argument here is the fact that the function
I(\cdot)
is identically
0
on the interval
[0,R_{p}]
, this will be used repeatedly. If
0\notin\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
, then there exists
x\in[0,a_{M}]
such that
F_{\theta,\nu}(x)<F_{\theta,\nu}(0)
. Since
F(x)=W(\theta(1-x))+\theta\nu^{-1}I(\nu x)
, by continuity, there exists
\nu^{\prime}<\nu
such that
F_{\theta,\nu^{\prime}}(x)>F_{\theta,\nu}(x)=F_{\theta,\nu^{\prime}}(x)
. Thus, this tells us that
\nu>\nu_{c}
, a contradiction. Now, suppose
\mathscr{M}(\theta,\nu)=\{0\}
. Then let
F_{**}(\nu)=\min_{x\geqslant R_{p}/2\nu_{c}}F(x)
.
F_{**}(\nu)
is continuous in
\nu
by definition. Note,
F_{**}(\nu)>F(0)
. In particular, there exists a sufficiently small perturbation
\nu^{\prime\prime}>\nu
such that
F_{**}>F(0)
. Since changing
\nu
does not alter the behavior of
F
on the interval
[0,R_{p}/2\nu_{c}]
,
0
remains the sole minimizer of
F_{\theta,\nu^{\prime\prime}}
, implying that
\nu<\nu_{c}
, a contradiction. Finally,
0
is isolated since
W(\theta(1-x))
is strictly increasing for all
x>0
.

\blacksquare

We expect
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
to be a singleton, however we currently lack certain properties of the function
I
required to prove this. In addition, when
\theta<C_{d}
, we have
F^{\prime}(0)>0
while
F^{\prime}(x)=0
for all other minimizers. This has an important consequence, and the intuition provided by the saddle point method suggest that the local limit should be the same as that of the super critical case where mass is lost. It seems that a non trivial mixture can be observed when
\theta=C_{d}
, however this would require
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
to be finite, and for the second derivatives of
F
at all
x\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
to be positive. We currently lack this. In addition, we would require a refinement of the rate of error in the convergence of the free energy, the current rate is inadequate for the analysis by Laplace asymptotics. It is unclear whether the same discontinuity of phase transition holds for
\theta\geqslant C_{d}
.

3.

Fluctuations near
U
for
\nu>\nu_{c}
:
In this article, we have proved that the fluctuations of
\Psi_{N}^{\theta,\nu}
are
O(1)
and asymptotically Gaussian far away from
U
. However, we anticipate that these properties should hold in the vicinity of
U
as well. We reserve this as a direction of future research.

4.

Same result for
p\leqslant 4
:
When
p\leqslant 4
, notice that the dyadic argument breaks down because there is no threshold beyond which the non linear part can be ignored. We can still prove the required
\ell^{\infty}
estimate to start the argument, but cannot bootstrap it down enough to drown out the non linearity. New additions to our techniques are likely required.

7.1

KK would like to thank Marek Biksup for an enlightening discussion on the relation of the model under consideration to the self attracting random walk, and the Salmon Coast Field Station for hosting him while a portion of this work was completed.
